#5960. Democratic senator-elect calls Trump 'unserious fool' while defending combative political style

Michigan Sen.-elect Abdul El-Sayed mocked Vice President JD Vance and President Donald Trump in his victory speech.

Abdul El-Sayed, the Democratic senator-elect from Michigan, leaned into accusations of acting cocky on the path to victory over Republican candidate Mike Rogers, citing a need to push back on President Donald Trump’s manner of communication during an appearance on "Pod Save America."

"Long ago, you predicted that you would win this race by seven points, which, at the time, seemed like a pretty cocky and ill-considered thing to say. Then you won the race by seven points. People are still calling you cocky," podcast host Jon Favreau said during his interview with El-Sayed.

"I think there is a responsibility that we have to engage with [Trump] as the unserious fool that he is," El-Sayed replied.

EL-SAYED MOCKS TRUMP, VANCE AND ROGERS IN GLOATING VICTORY SPEECH SIGNALING COMBATIVE TONE IN SENATE

Abdul El-Sayed, the Democratic nominee for U.S. Senate, speaks at the Michigan Democratic Nominating Convention in Lansing, Mich., Aug. 29, 2026. (Anadolu via Getty Images)

El-Sayed’s response sheds light on his view of his own campaign and the tone that he believes Democrats need to adopt to counter Trump’s presence in American politics. It also raises questions of whether El-Sayed’s campaign strategies will yield results in Congress.

In his view, Trump has changed the style of acceptable communication. In turn, he accused Democrats of being hesitant to abandon a kind of decorum to fight fire with fire.

"I just think there’s a responsibility for us to walk out of 'The West Wing' episode that our politics used to feel like and into the reality of where we are," El-Sayed said, referring to a television drama known for its idealistic portrayal of American politics.

"I say that because if you’ve ever watched a bully in a room, if you don’t stand up to the bully, it’s not like the bully will just go away. The bully just gets stronger."

During his campaign, El-Sayed repeatedly pointed a finger at Trump for, in his view, ushering in a new age of political rhetoric. But that view also preceded his Senate bid.

TRUMP DOMINATES EL-SAYED'S ATTACKS AGAINST ROGERS, BUT GOP RIVAL OFFERS UNEXPECTED POLICY SPLIT

President Donald Trump delivers remarks during a campaign rally at the Ocean Center Arena Oct. 30, 2026, in Daytona Beach, Fla. (Andrew Harnik/Getty Images)

He once accused President Donald Trump of inciting terrorism in a 2020 interview, citing a foiled plot to kidnap Gov. Gretchen Whitmer.

"These are terrorists; we have to call them that. We have to recognize that that is what the face of terrorism looks like right now. We have to step up against it," El-Sayed said.

"We gotta ask: Why are these people getting radicalized? Well, there must be some radical, extremist cleric out there who may be tweeting up a storm about all the things that they think need to be done, like liberating Michigan."

Although President Donald Trump did not call for any kind of violence against Whitmer, El-Sayed said he believed the president was at least partially responsible, pointing to a tweet Trump had made six months before the discovered plot.

"LIBERATE MICHIGAN!" Trump wrote in a post on Twitter, the social media platform now known as X. The post was one of a series calling to "liberate" Virginia and Minnesota from COVID-19 restrictions imposed by Democratic governors.

According to FBI comments to local media, the plot originated before Trump's post.

Even so, El-Sayed called the posts emblematic of a cultural toxicity that had made violence more likely.

"The idea that people would threaten her safety because she was willing to stand up to keep us all healthy is, to me, the example of what the kind of toxicity that we see out of Donald Trump creates," El-Sayed said.

MAJORITY LEADER THUNE SAYS SENATE GOP WON'T 'STAND IDLY BY' AS DEMOCRATS TAKE CONTROL OF CONGRESS

Abdul El-Sayed, the Democratic Senate nominee in Michigan, takes questions from reporters after a campaign rally Oct. 24, 2026 in Detroit. (Paul Steinhauser/Fox News Digital)

A spokesperson for El-Sayed did not immediately respond to requests for comment on whether he believed in a red line for what kind of rhetoric Democrats should refrain from using.

Having won his U.S. Senate election, El-Sayed will be sworn into office Jan. 3, 2027, the start of the 120th Congress.

Leo Briceno is a politics reporter for the congressional team at Fox News Digital. He was previously a reporter with World Magazine.

#5958. The Limits of China’s Self-Sufficiency

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

JOEL WUTHNOW is a Senior Research Fellow at the Institute for National Strategic Studies at the National Defense University and a co-author of China’s Quest for Military Supremacy. The views expressed here are his own.

Why the Country’s Food Insecurity Could Prevent a War Over Taiwan

When assessing the probability of a conflict in the Taiwan Strait, analysts tend to focus on a few obvious risks that China would face—namely, military defeat, economic slowdown, and restricted oil and technology imports. All of these are important, but an equally significant vulnerability is often left off the list: China’s reliance on soybean imports.

Beijing recognizes this liability. At the 20th Party Congress, in 2022, Chinese leader Xi Jinping identified “ensuring the security of food, energy resources, and critical industrial supply chains” as issues essential to national security and social stability. Food security, conspicuously listed first, is an especially sensitive issue for China’s 1.4 billion inhabitants: roughly 15 percent of the country’s population today was alive during the 1959–61 famine, which resulted in an estimated 30 million deaths.

As with technology and oil, Beijing has tried to limit its dependence on soybeans by building up stockpiles and diversifying its sources. But because soybeans are a fragile foodstuff, there is only so much China can do. Beijing can support a war effort, therefore, for only as long as the Chinese public could put the People’s Liberation Army’s success above its nutritional needs. And the key for deterring Chinese leaders from starting a conflict is to convince them that a domestic crisis would arrive sooner than any battlefield victory.

Disruption to critical imports is one of the most powerful deterrents to China’s use of force, and it’s a threat that the Chinese government has spent decades working to counter. The specter of a foreign blockade in the Strait of Malacca, through which most of Chinese oil imports pass, has worried Chinese leaders since at least 2003, when Chinese leader Hu Jintao warned about the risk of major powers’ interference creating a “Malacca dilemma” for China’s energy security. Beijing, however, has stockpiled massive reserves of oil, which it could complement in a crisis by increasing imports of Russian oil and, as Moscow has done, building up a sanctions-evading shadow fleet to transport oil from the Middle East.

Access to foreign technology is a newer but just as important concern, and China is similarly stockpiling critical components, such as chips. It is also having success in developing local alternatives. Beijing has already pulled ahead in some sectors, such as solar and battery manufacturing, or it is working to close the gap in others, as in semiconductors.

But Beijing has fewer options when it comes to self-sufficiency in food. Domestic production of staple grains—such as wheat, rice, and corn—accounts for more than 90 percent of consumption in these categories, but China remains dependent on foreign producers for other important foodstuffs, including soybeans, beef, and barley. Between 2000 and 2020, the share of China’s food supply provided by imports grew from six percent to 34 percent, and analysts predict it to increase to 41 percent by 2030. Part of this growth comes from the country’s economic development; as people become wealthier, their diets diversify, and they are no longer satisfied eating only grains. Between 1962 and 2024, meat as a percentage of the typical Chinese diet increased by a factor of 18.

This is where the central role of soybeans comes in. The legume provides a complex array of proteins in animal feed that other food sources and synthetic amino acids cannot replicate. Without it, livestock herds in China would shrink, thereby threatening domestic meat and dairy supplies. Soybean oil is also the most used cooking oil in China, making up nearly half the market. Alternatives, such as palm and rapeseed oils, are also mostly imported and face the same risks of maritime disruption, offering little relief.

China has invested a great deal in securing and, when possible, diversifying its access to foreign food sources. In 2013, a Chinese company bought Smithfield Foods, the United States’ largest pork producer. And state-owned enterprises such as COFCO International, a branch of China’s largest food and agriculture company, are buying farms and building logistical infrastructure in major agriculture-producing countries, such as Argentina and Brazil, which allows them to buy directly from farmers. Such direct investments provide a buffer against price volatility, giving Beijing more control over the volume and cost of supplies at their source. China has also steadily increased its own domestic meat production, which accounts for more than 90 percent of China’s demand. But such efforts do little to mitigate the effects of disruptions in maritime shipping—especially of soybeans, which is a supply chain that is fragile even in peacetime.

The central rub is that China has no choice but to import its soybeans. There is not enough arable land in China to both expand soybean cultivation and sustain self-reliance in staple grains. (Soybeans are also a much less efficient crop than corn or wheat in China’s arid north, making this tradeoff the economical choice.) But the countries that grow and export soybeans are almost all in the Western Hemisphere, which presents even more logistical challenges. When the trade war between the United States and China broke out in 2018, for instance, Beijing limited its U.S. soybean imports from 40 percent to about 20 percent of its supply, making up the difference with increased imports from Brazil. But Brazil’s poor infrastructure and frequent natural disasters make its harvest more susceptible to failure than the U.S. harvest. In 2024, one Brazilian state lost a quarter to a third of its soybean harvest because of flooding.

U.S. and Brazilian ports are also about as far from China as they could be. Brazil’s Santos port, for example, is more than 10,000 miles from Guangzhou, which takes 45 days to ship. Because of soybeans’ sensitivity to humidity, this distance means that ships often arrive in China with rotten cargo. For similar reasons, the legume is also difficult to stockpile. Unlike rice and wheat reserves, which Beijing claims could last for years, soybean reserves are smaller and must be replaced more frequently. Chinese stockpiles might be able to last only a few months if its imports were cut off. Although China has been experimenting with bioengineered supplements in an effort to reduce the amount of soybeans in animal feed, rising food demand is offsetting any gains. In other words, its dependence on soybean imports is only continuing to grow.

The outbreak of war between China and the United States would pose existential threats to this supply chain. In the most extreme scenario, the U.S. military could block shipments from the Western Hemisphere to China, which would take away most of the country’s soybean imports. China has no naval bases or forward forces in Latin America that could counter these efforts.

But China’s food supply chains would suffer from disruption even without a U.S. embargo or blockade. Any war in the Pacific would bring with it massive risk premiums for maritime shipment insurance that might cause shipping firms to balk. Such an effect has already been on display for shipping in the Red Sea, where Houthi missile threats have caused insurance costs to skyrocket since 2024. A war in the Pacific could be far more intense and costly because it would involve major navies, and ships could not route around the danger zone, making the long-haul journey from Brazil financially ruinous for many companies.

Beijing could, in theory, offer sovereign guarantees to offset firms’ insurance premiums. Even then, the firms transporting soybeans, most of which are owned by U.S. and allied country parties, might cease transporting products to China to avoid abetting a hostile regime, to steer clear of dangers that would put their employees at risk, and to head off possible future exposure to sanctions for doing business with the country.

Chinese analysts believe that China could circumvent some of these risks by using its own commercial fleet. Although this fleet has impressive capacity during peacetime—the Chinese shipping giant COSCO owns 468 dry bulk carriers—in wartime it would be stretched thin, facing demands to help move construction materials, industrial supplies, and foodstuffs for the People’s Liberation Army and to transport critical military materials such as coal and iron ore. Even available ships would likely lack personnel with the training needed to keep soybeans fresh during the long journey. And if ships tried to avoid the most dangerous sea-lanes, trips could take even longer, surpassing the 40 days that soybeans can be safely stored. Cargo might arrive rotten, if it arrived at all.

A war in the Pacific would not trigger an immediate food crisis in China. In the shorter term, the government could manage reduced imports by releasing supplies of soybeans, frozen pork, and other staples from state reserves. It could also reduce the minimum soybean inclusion rates for animal feed, which would slow livestock growth and cut meat output but which would be workable for a time. A few months of a managed crunch, however, is a best-case scenario. More likely is that panic buying and shipping disruptions would drain reserves faster than planned, turning a manageable shortfall into rising prices and visible shortages within months.

There is reason to believe, for instance, that Chinese stockpiles might not provide the short-term security they purport to. Over the last decade, local government warehouses meant to store emergency food supplies have been found empty or filled with rotten supplies, largely because of corruption. This not only calls into question how much extra food China truly has but also undermines public confidence in the government. In the case of a conflict, citizens conscious of the long-term disruption to come would begin panic buying and hoarding supplies. Whatever reserves do exist would eventually run out, with members of the elite likely being prioritized.

If the conflict dragged on, the situation would become increasingly dire. Agricultural producers, especially small farms, would be forced to cull their herds. Poultry, which requires especially high soybean content in its feed, would be the first casualty, which would then threaten the egg supply. Pork would be next. Cooking oils would grow scarce and expensive, making it difficult for restaurants and households to function. Eventually, public patience would wear thin: hoarding, protests over prices and shortages, and local officials diverting supplies could put mounting pressure on Beijing to end the war.

Unlike China’s vulnerabilities in energy or technology, the fragilities of the food system are endemic. If a conflict were to break out in the Pacific, the United States would not need to institute a blockade against China’s agricultural supplies or gather an international coalition to sanction China’s soybean imports. Soaring insurance costs and ship owners’ reluctance to sail into a war zone would be enough to squeeze the flow, and the Chinese government would have little recourse to stop it.

If Chinese leaders believed that they could achieve victory quickly and with few shots fired, they might take the bet. Deterrence, then, will require convincing them that they cannot. The United States, Taiwan, and other governments should invest in drones, rebuild munitions stockpiles, and ramp up messaging to China that a long, drawn-out conflict is likely. As long as China’s leaders believe that a war would be indecisive in its early stages, the threat of domestic crisis would be a powerful reason not to start one.

To Catch Up to China, America and Its Allies Must Dig

Beijing’s Theory of Success in the Face of Failure

#5957. A German Movie Previews Its Far-Right Future

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Review: A German Movie Previews Its Far-Right Future

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

Contemporary cinema from Germany seems determined to disabuse anyone—including Germans—who still imagines it as a nation of Birkenstock-wearing, anti-nuclear peaceniks who shrink from their own shadows. A spate of new, sharply edged films deftly take up where the socially critical New German Cinema of the 1970s—featuring directors such as Rainer Werner Fassbinder, Volker Schlöndorff, and Margarethe von Trotta—left off. This new Germany is something much darker than that portrayed in lighthearted films from recent decades like Fack ju Göhte and Good Bye, Lenin!, and the far right’s stunning results in September’s regional elections portend how much darker it may still become.

One of these unflinching filmmakers is the German Iranian director Faraz Shariat, whose Prosecution is a harrowing thriller about a young German lawyer with Korean roots—a lesbian named Seyo Kim (Chen Emilie Yan)—who starts as a state prosecutor in a small eastern German city. After winning three prizes at the Berlinale film festival in February, the arthouse thriller was released in Germany over the summer and was described by one reviewer as “a film of the political moment.”

Contemporary cinema from Germany seems determined to disabuse anyone—including Germans—who still imagines it as a nation of Birkenstock-wearing, anti-nuclear peaceniks who shrink from their own shadows. A spate of new, sharply edged films deftly take up where the socially critical New German Cinema of the 1970s—featuring directors such as Rainer Werner Fassbinder, Volker Schlöndorff, and Margarethe von Trotta—left off. This new Germany is something much darker than that portrayed in lighthearted films from recent decades like Fack ju Göhte and Good Bye, Lenin!, and the far right’s stunning results in September’s regional elections portend how much darker it may still become.

One of these unflinching filmmakers is the German Iranian director Faraz Shariat, whose Prosecution is a harrowing thriller about a young German lawyer with Korean roots—a lesbian named Seyo Kim (Chen Emilie Yan)—who starts as a state prosecutor in a small eastern German city. After winning three prizes at the Berlinale film festival in February, the arthouse thriller was released in Germany over the summer and was described by one reviewer as “a film of the political moment.”

It opens with a tense courtroom scene in which the freshly appointed Kim treats a racist attack on a Black citizen as a petty crime and not a politically motivated assault. She is the proud representative of Germany’s institutional justice, which she believes is “the most objective in the world.”

But her attitude changes quickly. After reprimanding a neo-Nazi defendant, she is deliberately knocked off her bike while cycling through a park by a hooded man beneath a bridge. From above, Molotov cocktails rain down, one of which explodes on her back, setting her jacket ablaze and burning her neck and jaw. The attackers flee yelling, “Take that, cunt!” Despite her injuries, Kim remains at the crime scene to ensure that forensic evidence is properly secured. She’s the victim now, and she’s determined to obtain justice in a system that isn’t objective, and perhaps never was.

Kim’s lawyer, Alexandra Tiedemann (played by Julia Jentsch), is the same lawyer who represented the Black citizen in the first scene, and raised the objection (then against Kim) that the German system is “blind in the right eye.” In other words, the establishment refuses to treat right-wing extremism with the same severity it does other politically motivated acts. Kim tells Tiedemann that, had Muslims been suspected of the attack on her, it would immediately have been labeled terrorism and all of the state’s resources brought to bear. This charge of official partiality has long been made by the German left—going all the way back to the 1950s when the West German justice system was stacked with former Nazis, the topic of Lars Kraume’s The People vs. Fritz Bauer (2015). But that was a long, long time ago, right?

Actor Burghart Klaussner as Fritz Bauer in The People vs. Fritz Bauer (2015).Martin Valentin Menke/zero one film GmbH

Racist hate crimes, critics like Shariat charge, are all too often either not prosecuted at all or treated as apolitical infractions. This spares perpetrators stiff sentences and hides Germany’s ugly secret: Eighty years after Nazi rule, a potent fascist network runs across the country, in all directions, not just in former East Germany. This leads to a sobering conclusion, namely that contemporary Germany has failed at its most elemental undertaking: ensuring that fascism never again flourishes on German soil.

Germany’s highest profile institutional failure (so far) were the murders committed by a neo-Nazi cell called the National Socialist Underground (NSU) between 2000 and 2007. The group murdered a German policewoman and nine people from primarily Turkish backgrounds. Despite evidence that piled up over those years, the NSU continued to kill, its murders constituting the deadliest extremist killing spree in postwar Germany at the time. Instead of homing in on the widespread neo-Nazi networks, the authorities targeted the victims’ families. As depicted in The Barber and the Bomb (a 2015 documentary by Andreas Maus), the German authorities falsely claimed the murders were tied to Turkish organized crime or gambling mafias. German authorities only identified the killers after they found a video made by the NSU claiming responsibility for the murders, bombings, and robberies.

A protester holds a sign with pictures of victims of the National Socialist Underground terrorist group, seen in front of the Higher Regional Court in Munich, Germany, on July 11, 2018.Matthias Balk/Getty Images

In Prosecution, Kim believes that the state’s institutions of justice can and will function, if worked the right way. (In the 2017 German film In the Fade, directed by Fatih Akin, the protagonist gives up on the justice system; she kills her family’s murderers and herself with a homemade bomb.) Nevertheless, Kim doesn’t count on the state to protect her. She arms herself and buys a jet-black, bulletproof sports car. As her legitimate quest for justice becomes an obsession, she crosses lines that make the film more than a black-and-white tale of good versus evil. Does she really have to break the law to make the law work? Do Germans with darker skin tones need to be armed on the street? Does Kim selfishly put others in harm’s way to convict her attackers?

As Kim immerses herself in the case—against the warnings of her superiors—she undertakes the investigative legwork that the city’s authorities themselves should do be doing but aren’t. Secure in her standing as a German professional, she refuses to act the way many non-German victims do: passive and defenseless, cowed by Germany’s intimidating bureaucracy. At night, Kim sneaks into the city’s archives to piece together evidence that reveals a dense far-right network that penetrates deep into the justice and public-security organs. She tracks down previous victims whose cases were swept under the rug—obvious leads not followed up, pertinent questions unasked, connections to other similar crimes unaddressed.

The obstacles that Kim encounters again and again point to sympathy for the far right beyond city hall’s custodial staff and a handful of police. Her superior, a senior prosecutor—an urbane, educated, reasonable-looking man—tells her that 80 percent of cases like hers go unsolved, and hers very well might too. He’s not a beer hall lowlife or neo-Nazi thug, and that’s exactly what’s so disturbing: The institutions of justice belie their neutral and orderly veneers. Germany’s far right wear ties, drive Porsches, possess law degrees. “Yeah, there are Germans around here who think like this,” a colleague tells her. “They can’t be excluded from public service. There’s no law.” The threat to people of color in Germany no longer lies exclusively outside the institutions but inside them as well.

Actress Diane Kruger as Katja Sekerci in the film In the Fade (2017).Magnolia Pictures

The film’s German title, Staatsschutz (literally “state protection”), does work for it that the English translation misses. In Germany, the federal and state-level Staatsschutz is the police organ responsible for defending the constitutional order, state institutions, and national security. This object of prosecution is unnamed, but democratic Germany protects its citizens from the likes of terrorism. But switch out the object and the state could be protecting another group: the perpetrators. What it protects above all else is itself: its own institutions, personnel, reputation. An image stained by far-right xenophobes is the last thing that Germany (of all countries) wants, so it’s not in the state’s interest to make federal cases out of racist and neo-Nazi crimes, neither in the former West or East.

Prosecution is all the more chilling today. It portrays conditions before the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) racked up 44 percent of the vote in the eastern German state of Saxony-Anhalt in September and then 38 percent in another eastern state two weeks later. These votes constitute seismic events in postwar Germany, but Prosecution reveals the extent to which the far right has crept into German institutions without ever being voted into them. Regularly and for years now, German media has reported on cases of far-right chat groups in police units, neo-Nazi cliques in the armed forces, and AfD-affiliated judges and prosecutors. And as we see in Prosecution, for people of color and the LGBTQ community, that’s scary enough. In 2025, right-wing extremists committed 42,544 crimes across Germany, according to interior ministry statistics, including 8,396 explicitly xenophobic hate crimes—and these are the numbers of a system blind in the right eye. Critics suspect they are much higher.

Read More A black-and-white film still of actors Paul Lukas (left), Robert Ryan, and Merle Oberon walking among the rubble of destroyed buildings in “Berlin Express” (1948). A black-and-white film still of actors Paul Lukas (left), Robert Ryan, and Merle Oberon walking among the rubble of destroyed buildings in “Berlin Express” (1948). Filming Among the Rubble of Nazi Germany How Hollywood captured the struggle to make a better world. Analysis | Phil Tinline

How Hollywood captured the struggle to make a better world.

How threatening will life in an AfD-led Saxony-Anhalt be for the far right’s enemies? One has to assume significantly so. And for this reason, many who fall on the far right’s undesirable list are sitting on packed bags, report German media. In an AfD-governed state, the well-being of people of color and the LGBTQ community will likely be distinctly worse than that of Kim in Prosecution. With the justice and interior ministers in its portfolio, an AfD government will control the Staatsschutz and the state intelligence agency, too—the latter of which currently classifies the AfD as “right-wing extremist.” So, too, will it control police personnel, and thus the department’s priorities and deployment, as well as asylum and deportation rules in the state. Ministers appoint political civil servants such as state secretaries and department heads. The justice ministry gives instructions to public prosecutors like Kim.

The AfD and its cronies already set the tone in bastions like Saxony-Anhalt, and have done so for years. But now the hatred bears a stamp of legitimacy, even if it violates the law. Under the wing of the AfD, perpetrators like those who fire-bombed Kim in Prosecution will surely feel emboldened—and protected.

In pondering the larger issue of how this can happen in Germany (a country long considered a pioneer in coming to terms with the past), one has to watch another contemporary German film, Fatherland, directed by Pawel Pawlikowski. The black-and-white biopic follows the great German writer Thomas Mann as he and his daughter, Erika, who have been living in the United States, tour eastern and western Germany in 1949, just as the country’s postwar division is solidifying. In Frankfurt, in the West, the state is stocked with former Nazis. In the East, the Soviet system, with all of its flaws, is assuming shape. There’s something profoundly wrong and disturbing at this birth moment of the two Germanys, and the Manns sense it. These birth defects resonate to today.

Paul Hockenos is a Berlin-based journalist. His recent book is Berlin Calling: A Story of Anarchy, Music, the Wall, and the Birth of the New Berlin.

#5954. House GOP critics erupt at Stephen Miller as Trump elevates him after midterm massacre

Fox News senior White House correspondent Peter Doocy reports on President Donald Trump's announcement that Stephen Miller will replace Susie Wiles as White House chief of staff on ‘America Reports.’

Several Republican lawmakers are fuming over President Donald Trump’s elevation of Stephen Miller to be his chief of staff.

The fiercely loyal Miller, who has been with Trump since his first administration, took over for Susie Wiles, who was ousted from the critical role on Thursday. While he has plenty of admirers, his take-no-prisoners style has made him an enemy of Democrats and even some Republicans.

"Horrible decision," Rep. Marlin Stutzman, R-Ind., told Fox News Digital Friday after Trump announced his decision.

A Republican lawmaker who was granted anonymity to speak candidly described being in "utter disbelief."

Tensions were rising against Miller in the wake of Republicans’ terrible performance in Tuesday’s midterm elections. Multiple House Republicans who spoke with Fox News Digital just moments before his promotion said Miller, who spearheads Trump’s immigration policy, should be fired.

TRUMP NAMES STEPHEN MILLER NEW WHITE HOUSE CHIEF OF STAFF, REPLACING SUSIE WILES

White House deputy chief of staff for policy Stephen Miller outside the White House Oct. 6, 2025. (Aaron Schwartz/CNP/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

When later asked about his promotion, one House Republican who said Miller should be fired replied simply with a facepalm emoji.

Republicans suffered historic losses that saw both the House and Senate fall into Democrats’ hands in the new Congress. Democrats are on pace to win more than 250 seats in the House and at least 54 in the Senate.

AMERICA SAW THE BLUE WAVE COMING, BUT DRASTICALLY UNDERESTIMATED THE EXTENT OF THE DEM TSUNAMI

And while voter analyses have shown that Trump’s war in Iran and tariffs played the biggest roles in turning people away from the GOP, his handling of illegal immigration served to alienate voters in key areas. That’s in no small part due to Hispanic and Latino voters turning their backs on Republicans in places like Texas that shifted massively toward Democrats in this election cycle.

"Because of his immigration positions, I just think we ruined a lot of our goodwill with Latino voters," Stutzman said.

The second GOP lawmaker told Fox News Digital that Miller ignored multiple warnings from House Republicans in the past, arguing that Trump’s immigration crackdown is what voters asked for.

"I've been in several group meetings where a lot of us have told him, ‘Your immigration enforcement policies are backfiring back home. Our people are mad, our businesses are mad — even in deep red districts — and it is impacting not just the voters, but the donors, and everybody top to bottom," that GOP lawmaker said.

"But it looked to me like Stephen Miller was only after numbers. … He would not listen to us."

Other Republicans who spoke with Fox News Digital also criticized Miller but did not go as far as to say he should be fired.

Rep. Marlin Stutzman, R-Ind., attends a House Financial Services Committee hearing May 7, 2025. (CQ-Roll Call, Inc. via Getty Images)

REPUBLICANS TURN ON TRUMP AFTER DEVASTATING MIDTERM LOSSES HAND DEMOCRATS CONTROL OF CONGRESS

"I think he's greatly hurt the administration and cost us many seats on Tuesday," retiring Rep. Don Bacon, R-Neb., told Fox News Digital before news of Miller’s promotion broke.

"He took an immigration policy that favored the president 2:1 in the public, overreached and made it 2:1 unfavorable.

Miller is widely known as Trump’s right hand on immigration policy, including earlier this year when the administration faced bipartisan backlash over its handling of anti-immigration enforcement protests in places like Minneapolis.

Several Republicans, particularly those running in battleground districts, had called for Trump to limit deportations to illegal immigrants who have committed crimes while in the United States.

A fourth GOP lawmaker said, "Stephen is a megalomaniac who should have never been given the keys to the Ferrari. That being said, he's not the reason why we lost. ... No one knows who Stephen Miller is in our districts."

Various other administration officials also got their share of blame in the fallout from Tuesday’s midterm massacre, including Secretary of War Pete Hegseth and Trump himself.

But the president and people in his orbit have blamed candidate quality, arguing that, in many cases, the president campaigned harder than some of the people he supported did for themselves.

And in his Truth Social post announcing Miller’s promotion, Trump called him one of his "most trusted advisors." He later told reporters Miller would "do a great job."

Rep. Don Bacon, R-Neb., arrives at a House Republican Conference meeting in the U.S. Capitol Building July 21, 2026, in Washington, D.C. (Anna Moneymaker/Getty Images)

When Fox News Digital reached the White House for comment, spokeswoman Lauren Bis said Miller "has been a key player in delivering on President Trump’s America First agenda since day one."

"He has spearheaded the administration’s crime crackdown, including in D.C. and Memphis; record-setting deregulation; unleashed American energy; dismantled drug cartels; and secured our border. In his new role, he will continue to deliver historic successes for the American people," Bis said.

Bis also pointed to a list of positive comments about Miller by administration officials and members of Congress.

"Very grateful to Susie for her friendship and service to the president and our country. Stephen Miller is an amazing patriot and will do great things. Congrats to him on a well deserved promotion!" Vice President JD Vance said.

"Stephen Miller has built a proven record focused on securing our border, combating crime and advancing a now much-needed commonsense conservative agenda for our country," House Freedom Caucus Chairman Andy Harris said.

A White House official told Fox News Digital Miller had experience working with Democrats on the D.C. Safe and Beautiful Task Force and that he has been a "valuable, trusted aide" since 2016.

#5952. America’s AI Ambitions Depend on Taiwan

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

LIN CHIA-LUNG is Minister of Foreign Affairs of Taiwan.

Washington Cannot Achieve Tech Dominance on Its Own

The United States and China are locked in a global competition to determine who will dominate artificial intelligence and whether it will be democracies or autocracies that set the standards and values of the new digital age. The United States leads the world in the sophistication and power of its artificial intelligence models, and its innovation base and investment in research and development remain unparalleled.

But AI competition is much broader than modeling alone. It encompasses a technology ecosystem that includes power generation, servers, and deployment in real-world applications. Jensen Huang, co-founder and CEO of Nvidia, has described the AI industry as a five-layer cake with energy at its base and chips, infrastructure, models, and applications above it. To win the AI race with China, the United States needs to maintain or establish an advantage in each of these layers. And to do that, the United States needs Taiwan.

Taiwan produces more than 90 percent of the world’s most cutting-edge semiconductors, making it indispensable to the United States’ AI industry. Taiwanese companies also assemble roughly 90 percent of the servers that power the global AI economy. With complementary strengths in manufacturing and design, respectively, Taiwan and the United States have built a mutually reinforcing partnership in AI that has underpinned U.S. leadership in the sector. Alongside democratic countries around the world that don’t want to rely on China for their technology needs, the U.S.-Taiwanese partnership has also made a secure AI supply chain possible.

Some policymakers in the United States have blamed Taiwan for the offshoring of U.S. semiconductor manufacturing. Although it is true that the United States no longer manufactures as large a share of the world’s chips as it did in the mid-twentieth century, this narrative obscures the larger story. Taiwan did not weaken the U.S. semiconductor industry. Instead, Taiwan has been crucial to maintaining the United States’ technological superiority. By carving out a niche in advanced manufacturing and supply chain management, Taiwan can help the United States maintain its advantage in innovation and R & D. As the stakes of the AI race grow, Taipei and Washington—and the rest of the democratic world—will need to work together to prevail.

First and foremost, AI relies on access to advanced semiconductors. For much of the 1950s through the 1970s, the United States dominated the entire chip industry, from design to manufacturing. As the Center for Strategic and International Studies notes, “The United States created the modern-day semiconductor industry and has historically led in the technology’s development.” But as chips became increasingly complex and expensive to produce, the industry became more specialized. Although Silicon Valley held on to its strong lead in chip design, it began to outsource manufacturing capacity to East Asia. Today, as Washington tries to revive manufacturing, understanding the sources of this shift are essential for determining how to most effectively rebuild U.S. capacity.

In fact, Taiwan’s semiconductor industry was born out of U.S.-Taiwanese cooperation. With the onset of globalization and expansion of free trade starting in the 1970s, U.S. companies accelerated their efforts to outsource chip manufacturing to overseas partners. Taiwan’s government, seeking to move up the global value chain, established the Industrial Technology Research Institute, an organization for nurturing new technology ventures and channeling R & D to industries; bought technology licenses from the United States; and built Hsinchu Science Park in northwestern Taiwan. Taiwan also invested heavily in education and training for engineers. As the semiconductor industry developed, large Taiwanese technology firms such as TSMC expanded their operations and countless small and medium-size enterprises contributed to supply chains, creating a virtuous cycle that encouraged entrepreneurship, strengthened regulations and standards, and promoted international cooperation.

The result was an industrial ecosystem that was specialized, collaborative, and difficult to replicate. This ecosystem freed up the United States to do what it does best: design incredibly complex chips. Taiwan’s investment in manufacturing not only lowered the cost of U.S. technology; it also meant that whatever Silicon Valley dreamed up, Taiwan was soon capable of building.

Taiwan’s success as a semiconductor powerhouse has therefore never been at the expense of the United States. Rather, it has been based on decades of collaboration, a division of labor, and mutual trust between Washington and Taipei. Today, the United States continues to lead the world in semiconductor innovation, design, and research and development, whereas Taiwan is at the forefront of advanced manufacturing, packaging, and supply chain integration. U.S. tech firms such as Nvidia, Apple, and Qualcomm need Taiwan’s manufacturing sector to turn their creative ideas into usable products. Rather than replace the United States, Taiwan enables American innovation to reach global markets.

Still, Taiwan understands Washington’s determination to strengthen domestic semiconductor production and is willing to support that effort. TSMC has committed to investing $265 billion in semiconductor fabrication plants in Arizona. More than 20 other Taiwanese companies have also invested billions of dollars in the U.S. semiconductor ecosystem, creating jobs and expanding manufacturing capacity in the United States.

For Taiwan, these investments strengthen the existing supply chain partnership with the United States. They will become even more important as the U.S. semiconductor ecosystem grows, because the United States will need to rely on Taiwan’s knowledge and network of suppliers and engineers, built up over decades, to sustainably develop its own industry. There is no contradiction between what’s good for the United States and what’s good for Taiwan: with demand for chips continuing to rise, both sides benefit from deeper partnership and the sharing of expertise and supply chain security.

The U.S.-Taiwanese partnership extends beyond chips. The United States boasts world-leading AI platforms and cloud service providers; Taiwan is the world’s leading manufacturer of the AI servers on which they run. Taiwanese companies such as Foxconn, which accounts for approximately 40 percent of the world’s AI server market, and Quanta Computer, which builds high-density AI servers and infrastructure for cloud service providers, manufacture the essential products and components underpinning the operations of major U.S. technology companies, including Alphabet, Amazon, and Meta.

The United States can expand its AI-related manufacturing capacity, but it would take many years for it to match the scale, supplier networks, engineering expertise, and manufacturing capabilities of Taiwan’s existing industry. It is far easier and cheaper for Taiwan to manufacture the infrastructure that enables U.S.-designed AI chips and platforms to be deployed rapidly and at scale. Even if the United States were to drastically expand its domestic manufacturing capacity, it would only be able to produce a fraction of what is needed to keep pace with AI demand.

The next phase of AI competition will be over how to apply the technology in the real world. The United States and China are going to be in a race to fuse AI with robots, drones, and other defense systems, as well as to integrate it into manufacturing, health care, and transportation. Indeed, this process is already well underway: robotic taxis, for instance, are now operating in cities such as San Francisco and Shenzhen, and companies in China and the United States are jockeying for leadership in the sector.

In the application of AI technology, Taiwan’s strengths also complement those of the United States. The island’s semiconductor, communications equipment, and precision machinery industries are precisely those that will be needed to build the physical systems that can use American AI models. Taiwanese companies such as Thunder Tiger are already developing AI-driven defense drones, demonstrating the growing potential for integrating artificial intelligence, precision engineering, and advanced electronics into civilian and military applications. As AI evolves from a purely digital technology to an essential component of factories, robots, and vehicles, Taiwan’s advanced hardware production base will be even more valuable to the United States.

As the AI race intensifies, it will also become a contest over values, data management, and trust. If the United States and its democratic allies and partners want to stay ahead in technology, they must work together to create secure and trustworthy AI ecosystems that reflect their principles.

Supply chain collaboration will be crucial because no single place can dominate every aspect of the AI ecosystem. The United States excels in innovation and is home to major platforms; Japan is a leader in specialized refining, recycling, and research into deep-sea resources, as well as in precision semiconductor manufacturing equipment; the Netherlands supplies advanced lithography machines needed to produce the most advanced chips; and Taiwan specializes in wafer fabrication and packaging. Only through coordination can these countries build efficient and secure AI supply chains while reducing their reliance on China for essential components.

International disruptions have reinforced the necessity of supply chain cooperation among democratic countries. The COVID-19 pandemic, for instance, exposed the weaknesses of the world’s global supply chain networks, a lesson reinforced by disruptions to trade flows in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine and rising energy prices amid instability in the Middle East. In 2025, Taiwan proposed a global semiconductor democratic supply chain partnership initiative to jointly construct reliable systems that can withstand international disruptions. In this initiative, Taiwan seeks to harness cooperation among democratic countries to usher in a new era of growth in industries connected to high-end chips.

Taiwan and the United States have already made strides in technology cooperation. The U.S.-Taiwan Economic Prosperity Partnership Dialogue, a bilateral platform inaugurated in November 2020, has hosted high-level discussions on AI supply chain security, digital infrastructure, critical minerals, drone supply chains, and high-tech talent. These exchanges have helped U.S.-Taiwanese cooperation evolve beyond traditional trade and economic relations and paved the way for establishing a technological ecosystem that can bring together democratic partners and allies.

Taiwan also stands ready to ensure the safe and ethical usage of AI. Safety depends not only on the makeup of the AI model itself but also on the security and reliability of the hardware on which it operates—an area in which Taiwan can bring its expertise to bear. Taiwan and the United States can work together to increase cybersecurity, establish testing standards, and build reliable manufacturing systems to ensure that AI develops safely and can be trusted by the United States and its allies.

When it comes to the technology underpinning AI, Taiwan is not an adversary of the United States but its most trustworthy partner. In every layer of the AI “cake,” Taiwan and the United States have developed—and can continue to expand—a productive and mutually beneficial partnership. Taiwan is a force multiplier for the United States in artificial intelligence: it has the capability, reliability, and values necessary to support the United States’ technological ambitions. If Washington wants to compete effectively in AI, Taiwan is indispensable.

Use Today’s Calm to Prepare for the Next Perfect Storm

How Far Will He Go to Make Peace—and War?

Daniel W. Drezner and Elizabeth N. Saunders

#5951. The U.S. Senate Just Got a Lot Younger

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Infographic: The U.S. Senate Just Got a Lot Younger

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

The U.S. midterm elections on Tuesday saw the biggest Senate turnover in nearly 50 years, with at least 15 new lawmakers elected to join the upper chamber come January. Several of Capitol Hill’s octogenarians will be leaving the Beltway, and a whole batch of younger legislators will be moving in.

The United States once had one of the oldest average upper houses in the developed world, according to data from IPU Parline. As of this month, the average age of U.S. senators is 65.4 years old. Among members of the Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) for which data is available, the next eldest nation’s class is roughly five years younger, with Poland’s Senate having an average age of 61.9 years and the Netherlands’s Senate an average age of 58.4 years.

The U.S. midterm elections on Tuesday saw the biggest Senate turnover in nearly 50 years, with at least 15 new lawmakers elected to join the upper chamber come January. Several of Capitol Hill’s octogenarians will be leaving the Beltway, and a whole batch of younger legislators will be moving in.

The United States once had one of the oldest average upper houses in the developed world, according to data from IPU Parline. As of this month, the average age of U.S. senators is 65.4 years old. Among members of the Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) for which data is available, the next eldest nation’s class is roughly five years younger, with Poland’s Senate having an average age of 61.9 years and the Netherlands’s Senate an average age of 58.4 years.

But Washington’s new crop of senators will reduce that number by quite a significant margin. The average age of the 15 departing U.S. senators is 70 years old. Their replacements are coming in at roughly age 55. That will bring the average age of the U.S. Senate down to at least 62.8 years—still above most of its OECD counterparts but a dramatic shift nonetheless.

That also means that the Senate is moving toward closing the age gap between its members and the constituents they represent. According to the Economist, the United States had the least age-representative government within the OECD as of December 2024, with lawmakers more than 20 years older than the average population age. The outgoing class of senators has only eight members under age 50.

The new class, however, will nearly double that number, with at least 15 lawmakers under 50. Although the eldest age will remain at 93 (Republican Sen. Chuck Grassley of Iowa), the youngest age will go from 39 (Democratic Sen. Jon Ossoff of Georgia) to 37 (Democratic Sen.-elect James Talarico of Texas).

With more and more young people voting Democrat, according to Associated Press exit polls, it may explain why the upcoming Senate class also shifted younger. Still, only around 1 in 10 voters in the 2026 midterms were under 30.

#5950. California prosecutors slam Newsom's death row commutations, accuse governor of putting 'politics over people'

San Luis Obispo County District Attorney Dan Dow accused Gov. Gavin Newsom of ignoring crime victims and violating the will of California voters following a sweeping announcement on death row commutations.

District attorneys and law enforcement unions across California have publicly condemned Gov. Gavin Newsom following his announcement to commute the sentences of 548 death row inmates, a decision one prosecutor called a slap in the face to victims' families.

Newsom’s sweeping order to commute the condemned inmates to life in prison without the possibility of parole has drawn intense criticism alongside praise from anti-death penalty advocates.

"I think it's all about politics, sadly. Politics is over people in this case, and that's a real sad state of affairs," San Luis Obispo County District Attorney Dan Dow told Fox News Digital.

ALABAMA GOVERNOR COMMUTES DEATH SENTENCE AS DOUBTS EMERGE ABOUT EVIDENCE IN MURDER CONVICTION

Law enforcement officials and prosecutors have criticized Gov. Gavin Newsom for issuing a blanket commutation for all of California's death row inmates. (Jane Tyska/Digital First Media/East Bay Times via Getty Images/Frazer Harrison/WireImage)

In an open letter detailing his decision, Newsom said California's capital punishment system is fundamentally at odds with the state's principles.

"After nearly 30 years in public office, I have come to understand the limits of what government can justly accomplish," Newsom wrote. "I take this action because I firmly believe that state-sanctioned killing is incompatible with California’s values, undermines the foundations of our legal system, and diminishes each of us."

POPE LEO DENOUNCES DEATH PENALTY AS 'INADMISSIBLE' AHEAD OF PENTAGON'S PLANNED LIVESTREAMED EXECUTION

Upon taking office in 2019, Newsom issued an executive moratorium on capital punishment. California has not carried out an execution since 2006.

The backlash to the governor's announcement was immediate.

FLORIDA PUTS 77-YEAR-OLD MAN TO DEATH FOR DEPUTY'S MURDER AS STATE CLOSES IN ON EXECUTION RECORD

"Justice will not have been served for these victims, dozens of them in San Diego County," said Lt. Jared Wilson, president of the San Diego Police Officers Association.

"Their last memories were of these evil, evil men who took their lives. What Gavin Newsom has done is awful."

Lt. Sam Robinson calls to open a door at San Quentin State Prison's death row in San Quentin, Calif., Aug. 15, 2016. The prison is California's oldest penitentiary and houses the state's only death row for men with 700 condemned inmates. (Justin Sullivan/Getty Images)

Wilson and other law enforcement officials strongly criticized the blanket clemency, citing cases like that of Jesse Michael Gomez, who was sentenced to death for the 2016 murder of San Diego police officer Jonathan "J.D." De Guzman.

"Californians have been asked on two separate ballot initiatives to abolish the death penalty, and both times they have chosen to keep it for the most serious crimes," said Los Angeles County District Attorney Nathan Hochman.

"Until the law is changed, my office will continue to evaluate every death penalty-eligible case with the rigor, care and restraint these decisions demand."

NEW YORK BILLS COULD ABOLISH LIFE WITHOUT PAROLE FOR SERIAL KILLERS AND COP KILLERS, CRITICS WARN

Orange County District Attorney Todd Spitzer called the commutations "mercy for the wolves … while inflicting the ultimate act of cruelty on the sheep," saying voters in California have upheld the death penalty in recent years.

Riverside County Sheriff Chad Bianco, who previously ran for governor, noted that 127 inmates on California’s death row were sentenced for crimes committed in his county.

"These sentences were not handed down without due process," he said. "They followed extensive investigations, lengthy trials, evidence presented in court and decisions made by juries and judges.

"Yet, with one sweeping executive decision, the governor has chosen to override those outcomes, disregarding the voices of victims’ families, the work of prosecutors and the pursuit of justice that took years to achieve."

College students Rachel Newhouse and Aundria Crawford were killed separately. Their killer, Rex Krebs, was sentenced to death for the brutal slayings. (San Luis Obispo County District Attorney)

Among the 548 inmates whose sentences were commuted are two men convicted in San Luis Obispo County: Michael Whisenhunt, 61, who was sentenced to death for the 1995 torture and murder of his girlfriend's 1-year-old daughter, Kesha Gurke; and Rex Krebs, 60, who was convicted in the brutal slayings of two female college students.

CHARLES MANSON CULT FOLLOWER BRUCE DAVIS, CONVICTED IN TWO 1969 KILLINGS, DIES BEHIND BARS AT 83

Dow noted that families of the victims were neither consulted nor notified prior to Newsom's public announcement.

"I was just sickened, frankly, for the crime victims in California who were slapped in the face by our governor," Dow said.

JUDGE BLOCKS ALABAMA'S NITROGEN GAS EXECUTION METHOD, RULES IT IS UNCONSTITUTIONALLY CRUEL

Addressing arguments from anti-death penalty groups who contend that capital punishment is inherently flawed and racially biased, Dow defended the current legal framework.

"We have a rigorous defense procedure that weeds out cases. I have strong confidence in the integrity of our convictions," he said.

#5948. Global AI Stocks React to U.S. Midterm Results

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

World Brief: Global AI Stocks React to U.S. Midterm Results

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at how global AI stocks are reacting to the U.S. midterms, Pope Leo XIV’s tour of Latin America, and more hoax bomb threats across Europe.

Global stock markets in Europe and Asia plunged on Friday in the wake of big Democratic gains in this week’s U.S. midterm elections. Their reactions came a day after Wall Street saw massive sell-offs, as investors feared that Washington’s blue wave could bring tighter tech regulations for the artificial intelligence industry.

Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at how global AI stocks are reacting to the U.S. midterms, Pope Leo XIV’s tour of Latin America, and more hoax bomb threats across Europe.

Global stock markets in Europe and Asia plunged on Friday in the wake of big Democratic gains in this week’s U.S. midterm elections. Their reactions came a day after Wall Street saw massive sell-offs, as investors feared that Washington’s blue wave could bring tighter tech regulations for the artificial intelligence industry.

According to NBC exit polls, 57 percent of U.S. voters believed that AI would do more harm than good to society, and 56 percent of individuals characterized the tech’s controversial advancement as “crucial or a major factor” in determining who they voted for. Hoping to capitalize on that hesitance, many Democratic candidates had promised to rein in AI’s development and slow the construction of data centers. Many voters ahead of Tuesday’s election had expressed concerns about data centers’ impact on job creation, water usage, energy bills, and local pollution.

Some of Silicon Valley’s most influential executives, from SpaceX CEO Elon Musk to OpenAI co-founder Greg Brockman, collectively spent more than half a billion dollars backing Republican candidates. However, that didn’t appear to be enough to stop the U.S. midterms’ AI reckoning, FP’s Rishi Iyengar and Christina Lu wrote. Notably, several historically red states that host a vast number of data centers, such as Texas, swung blue.

This has made investors worried. On Thursday, the Nasdaq dropped by 5.52 percent (or roughly $2.5 trillion). The S&P 500 also fell by 4.36 percent, and the Dow Jones declined by 3.44 percent. All three index averages recorded their worst days since April 2025, when U.S. President Donald Trump launched his sweeping global trade war.

Such dips prompted foreign markets to react with matching concern. London’s FTSE 100 index sank by 2.35 percent on Friday, Germany’s Dax index tumbled by 2 percent, France’s Cac 40 decreased by 0.8 percent, and Spain’s Ibex declined by 0.6 percent. In the Indo-Pacific, Japan’s Nikkei 225 index dropped by 6 percent, and South Korea’s Kospi index fell by more than 6.5 percent.

Still, the initial market panic appears to have slowed for now. Wall Street began to recover on Friday from its mass stock sell-off, with the Nasdaq and S&P 500 both rallying. The rebound may be partly related to a new U.S. Labor Department report on Friday that saw the U.S. economy add 165,000 jobs in October.

Leo’s big trip. Pope Leo XIV kicked off a 12-day tour of Latin America on Friday, less than four weeks after undergoing surgery for lung cancer. The trip, which will see Leo deliver nine masses and 36 speeches across three countries, will test the pope’s health and stamina at a time when some observers have wondered whether papal succession talks need to begin sooner than expected. The Vatican, though, has made clear that Leo requires no special accommodations for this visit and will be accompanied by his usual delegation.

Leo will begin his trip in Uruguay before heading to Argentina and Peru. In Uruguay, Leo is expected to appeal to the country’s increasingly secular population; only 1 in 3 Uruguayans identify as Catholic, the lowest rate in Latin America. Leo has a history of prioritizing trips to countries with secular-leaning populations, such as his visit to France in September.

From Uruguay, Leo will head to Argentina, the homeland of his predecessor, Pope Francis. During that visit, the pope will meet with far-right Argentine President Javier Milei, whose policies have often run contradictory to the Vatican’s preachings. That includes Milei’s efforts to transform Argentina into an AI hub; Leo has made consideration of AI’s ethical implications an early focus of his papacy.

Finally, Leo will end his trip in Peru, where he spent a significant portion of his life. The pope is expected to travel to the city of Cusco, which sits at more than 11,000 feet above sea level. Thinner air quality there could be a challenge for the pope’s recovering lungs; however, the Vatican has stressed that Leo is healthy enough to do it.

More bomb threats. Another round of hoax bomb threats targeted Europe on Friday, adding to this week’s onslaught of suspected hybrid attacks on the continent. Newsrooms in Bulgaria and Ireland reportedly received emailed bomb threats from a “Mikhail Andropov” on Friday, forcing the media outlets to suspend programming and evacuate their offices. A British broadcaster also said that it had received a security alert, though it is unclear if that incident was linked to the other two.

Several European planes were also diverted on Friday due to false bomb threats—the third such incident this week. The fake threats have caused massive air traffic disruptions across the continent and upended travel plans for thousands of passengers. Friday’s incidents also came amid a surge in other types of hybrid warfare operations targeting Europe, including recent cyberattacks on supermarket refrigeration systems in at least 10 countries.

It remains unclear who is behind these attacks and if they are all related, but security officials suspect that Russia may be responsible, with analysts telling Reuters that the threats could be punishment for countries that have armed or financed Ukraine. Moscow continues to deny any involvement.

Chris vs. Chris. New Zealand voters head to the polls on Saturday for a closely watched general election defined by cost-of-living concerns. Analysts predict that the race will be a tight one. According to local estimates, the incumbent coalition led by the center-right National Party is polling at 26.4 percent, and the opposition left-leaning alliance led by the Labour Party is polling at 30.7 percent.

Whichever coalition wins a majority will likely put a Chris in power. Helming the National Party is Prime Minister Christopher Luxon, who has held power since 2023. Luxon is known for inking a free trade deal with India, implementing education reforms, and cracking down on gang violence; he is also known for verbal gaffes and appearing disconnected from ordinary people due to his millionaire status.

Leading Labour is Chris Hipkins. Having helped spearhead the country’s COVID-19 response as a cabinet minister under former Prime Minister Jacinda Ardern, Hipkins has pledged to reduce high prices and bolster healthcare funding. He has also been plagued by verbal gaffes (most notably, when he told people during the pandemic to “get outside and spread their legs”). If the National Party is ousted, it will have led New Zealand’s first one-term government in more than 50 years.

Brazil on Tuesday approved legislation that strengthened the government’s ability to do what?

A. Fight criminal gangs B. Cut back on deforestation C. Prosecute Flávio Bolsonaro D. Overcome presidential vetoes

It is perhaps the most divisive issue of our time: Should pineapple go on pizza? In May, Italian chef Davide Tamburella was fired from Australia’s now-shuttered La Costa restaurant for refusing to serve the topping, calling it an “insult to his culture.” This was the third time that Tamburella had refused to put the fruit on pizza in the past four years, among other complaints from management. On Tuesday, though, Australia’s Fair Work Commission dismissed Tamburella’s unfair termination claim on the grounds that his application had been filed too late.

Although he is fresh off an electoral victory, 81-year-old President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva will need to start cultivating a successor as soon as possible, Oliver Stuenkel writes.
