#5799. Hyundai Motor Group Outlines Future Mobility Technology Roadmap at FISITA World Mobility Summit 2026

HWASEONG, Korea — Hyundai Motor Group (the Group) is co-hosting the FISITA World Mobility Summit 2026 with the International Federation of Automotive Engineering Societies (FISITA). The two-day summit is taking place Nov. 4–5 at Rolling Hills Hotel in Hwaseong, Korea.

Founded in 1948, FISITA is a global organization that brings together automotive engineering societies and industry experts to promote knowledge exchange and technological innovation in automotive engineering. It holds the World Mobility Summit every two years, bringing together executives and technology leaders from global mobility companies to discuss industry challenges and future technology directions, such as artificial intelligence (AI) and vehicle electrification.

This year’s summit is the first to be held in Korea. Chang Hwan Kim, Executive Vice President and Head of the Electrification Energy Solutions Tech Unit at Hyundai Motor Group, is the first Korean to serve as FISITA President.

“Hyundai Motor Group will continue to strengthen its competitiveness in key future mobility technologies and continue to participate in technology exchange and collaboration across the global automotive industry to contribute to the development of the future mobility sector.” –Chang Hwan Kim, Executive Vice President and Head of the Electrification Energy Solutions Tech Unit at Hyundai Motor Group

On the first day, Manfred Harrer, President and Head of R&D Division at Hyundai Motor Group, delivered a keynote address introducing the Group’s electrification strategy. It encompasses battery electric vehicles (BEVs), hybrid electric vehicles (HEVs), extended-range electric vehicles (EREVs) and fuel cell electric vehicles (FCEVs). Harrer described the Group’s technology development roadmap for responding flexibly to market changes and customer needs.

Harrer also emphasized strengthening future vehicle development capabilities by combining engineering technology with AI-based digital capabilities and connecting the strengths of different technologies to pursue a sustainable mobility future.

In his opening remarks, Kim said that the automotive industry is undergoing an unprecedented technological transition. He emphasized that diverse technology approaches and stronger global collaboration are important to the advancement of future mobility.

Under the theme “Embracing the Complexities of Mobility,” the summit is addressing key technologies and challenges facing the automotive industry, including software-defined vehicles (SDVs), AI, autonomous driving, batteries and energy infrastructure.

Executives and technology leaders from across the global automotive industry are in attendance, sharing perspectives on key technology challenges and future development directions for the mobility industry.

Keynotes and panel discussions are examining major changes in the automotive industry, including advances in AI and software, the evolution of autonomous driving technology and the diversification of electrification technologies. Participants are also discussing technology directions and ways for the industry to collaborate in response to these shifts.

Major automakers are presenting vehicle and technology exhibits during the summit. The Group is displaying the Genesis GV90 and Hyundai TUCSON, along with a battery pack, hydrogen tank and robotics platform.

#5792. You Need A New Car? Go Electric! It’s Superior Technology! Part 1

Your car is getting “long in tooth,” or maybe your daughter is going away to college and you are giving her your car. Whatever the reason, you are looking for another car. It could be new, it could be used: don’t even look at gas cars, electric cars are superior technology!

The wheels on every railroad locomotive in the world are driven by electric motors. All the busiest rail routes in Europe and China are pure electric, including high-speed rail. 98% of new cars in Norway are electric, over 80% of new cars in Denmark are electric. In China, the country with the largest sales of new cars in the world, the percentage of new electric cars in September 2026 was 67%. 82% of the operating bus fleet in China is electric. Even ships and airplanes are going electric. The data is unequivocal: electric propulsion is superior to internal combustion propulsion. Only the huge political contributions to Trump by the fossil fuel industry and his resulting anti-EV policies have slowed down adoption in this country.

In places we’ve been like California; Portland, Oregon; and northern Utah, you are seeing a lot of Teslas, so you know that electric cars are working for a lot of people these days. It’s time for you to do some research on electric cars.

You do pay a premium for new electric cars, because the batteries are still quite expensive. If price is the driving factor for you, buy a used electric car. Hardly any used EVs are high mileage, and EV batteries still have plenty of life even after they go off warranty at 8 years and 120,000 miles. There are thousands of lightly used EVs sitting at used car lots at reasonable prices all over the country these days. Some used car dealers will give you an estimate of the battery life. If not, get a friend who is an experienced EV driver to help you evaluate the battery life of the EV you are considering.

If you just do local driving or have two cars and have a garage or off-street parking, an EV is a no brainer. EVs are hands-down more convenient than gas cars for local driving. Your EV will have plenty of range for local driving, it will get a complete charge overnight in your home garage refueling station and it will never need an oil change.

EVs are as quiet as mice, as smooth as kittens, and as quick as jackrabbits. They have simple electric motors, not complex internal combustion engines. They have simple one-speed transmissions, not complex 7-speed automatic transmissions. They don’t have exhaust systems with mufflers that will rust and need replacement. They don’t have emission control systems with limited life. They don’t have complex cooling systems with big radiators. You seldom use your brakes with an EV, so your brakes may last the life of the car. They don’t have a big lead-acid battery for starting, with a maximum life of 5 years. New EVs have only a small lithium-ion battery, which lasts 10+ years. Bottom line: Routine maintenance for EVs is almost nil. I think the only real maintenance item on a Tesla over the first 3 years is the cabin air filter. You can expect an EV to go 500,000 miles without major repair or replacement in most cases.

Perhaps you can stomach buying a car from Tesla whose CEO has become one of the most despicable persons on the planet lately. (Example: Musk recently suspended access to his Starlink communications system to the Ukrainians who are valiantly defending their country from the Russian aggressors.) However, Tesla has sold almost 10 million automobiles worldwide that don’t emit CO2 and particulate pollution while operating. Tesla also has by far the most ubiquitous charging system, which allows cross-country driving on major US roads with little planning. Consider buying a Tesla.

So, what about cross-country trips? Everyone knows that long-distance trips in an electric car are inconvenient or even impossible, because there aren’t enough charging stations and it takes an hour if you can find one. Wrong: Every 100 miles along all the major Interstate highways, Tesla has built Superchargers with 8 to 32 and a few with over 100 charging stalls. By the time you have gone to the bathroom, walked the dog, and chatted with a fellow EV owner for a few minutes, you probably have more than enough charge to continue your journey. Usually, charging takes about 20 minutes. If you need a longer charge, you may need to watch a bit of Octopus Teacher or Back to the Future II on the big screen in the car for another 10 minutes.

News flash! Since February 28, 2023, most Tesla Superchargers (now North American Charging Standard/NACS chargers) are usable by all EV brands. Unfortunately, older Tesla Superchargers can’t be used by other brands, so I would still advise buying a Tesla if you want carefree cross-country driving. CCS chargers which are native to most other brands have sprung up by the thousands in the US, which together with the newer Tesla Superchargers make it possible to travel cross-country with non-Teslas on many routes with some planning. Also, Teslas can use non-Tesla chargers. Non-Tesla chargers often come with Tesla cables now, so Tesla owners don’t even need an adapter in most cases. However, even Teslas require careful planning if you are driving far from major highways.

My wife and I have done cross-country trips from Utah to Wisconsin for 7 years, on to North Carolina (once), and back to Utah. We have also traveled from Utah to Southern California at the same 500 miles per day rate that we used to do with our gas cars … and with no worries, mate.

I’m going to try to pass on some things I’ve learned from driving five different electric vehicles over 12 years and some 200,000 miles. See part two, coming soon.

If you find any of my articles helpful to you, please use my referral link when buying a new Tesla: https://ts.la/arthur73734 (Be sure to use it when you make your order). Tesla keeps changing the program, but here’s the latest: If you are buying a new Tesla Model 3 or Y and use my link, you will get two months of free Full Self Driving (a ~$200 value) on top of the one month that comes free with every new Model Y right now. This will get you a total of 3 months free Full Self Driving. It will be $99/month after that. For a Cybertruck, you will get $1000 off your purchase price. At this moment, Tesla is offering a year of free Supercharging with the purchase of a new Model 3 Premium or Performance.

Arthur Frederick (Fritz) Hasler, PhD, former leader of NASA Goddard Space Flight Center Scientific Visualization & Analysis Laboratory (creator of this iconic image), and avid CleanTechnica reader. Also: Research Meteorologist (Emeritus) at NASA GSFC, Adjunct Professor at Viterbo University On-Line Studies, PSIA L2 Certified Alpine Ski Instructor at Brighton Utah Ski School.

#5789. You Need A New Car? Go Electric! It’s Superior Technology. Part 2

Some reasons why you shouldn’t be worried about buying an electric vehicle.

Everyone knows that electric cars are much more inconvenient than gas cars. After all, there is a gas station on every corner and it only takes 5 minutes to fill up. Electric chargers are rare, and it takes at least an hour to charge.

Wrong: If you have access to 220 volt electricity or a L2 charger at home or work, an electric vehicle is much more convenient than a gas car for 300 miles per day of local driving.

In principal: In the evening, you drive into your garage, carport or driveway and take 10 seconds to plug in, the next morning, you have a full charge and take 10 seconds to unplug.

Compare that with a gas car. You probably have to make a couple of special trips per week to the gas station, put in your credit card, grab the smelly nozzle, put it in your car, and wait five minutes to fill. Then you return the smelly nozzle to the pump.

With the gas car, every 3,000 miles, you need to go to a Jiffy Lube and sit in their smelly waiting room for an hour while they change your oil.

With the gas car, every year, you need to go to an inspection station and wait an hour in their smelly waiting room while they do an emissions check.

These three tasks are all eliminated with an electric vehicle, which makes it much more convenient for local driving.

How do you charge at home? Most electric cars come with an EVSE (Electric Vehicle Service Equipment) cable. If not, you can buy one for ~$200. On one end of the cable is the plug that goes into your car, and on the other end is an interchangeable plug. The one that comes with most cables is a standard 110V plug, but for $40 you can buy a 220V Nema 14-50 plug. Almost all RV parks have NEMA 14–50 sockets. If you have 220V service in your garage, you can have one installed for about $200. Northern Lights Electric installed one in my garage in Three Lakes Wisconsin for $130. An electrician in Saint George, Utah, installed one in our garage there for $250. It cost about $500 to have 220V service run to my garage in Lindon, UT. I had a L2 charger installed there for another $700, which I needed for my first Nissan LEAF in 2014. The L2 charger is not necessary if you have an EVSE, you only need a NEMA 14–50 outlet.

L2 charging gives you about 28 miles of range in one hour. Most EVs these days have more than 300 miles of range — so, in principle, you will charge from dead empty to full in ~11 hours. However, I am rarely at dead empty and I normally only charge to 80%, so usually I am only charging from one to five hours. Note: If you want your battery to last as long as possible, don’t charge over 80% or let the charge go below 20%. This is easier to do if you have a bigger battery.

L1 (110V) charging only gives you about 4 miles of range per hour. In principal, if you plug in next to your shaver in the bathroom, it would take three days for a full charge. However, if you drive fewer than 100 miles/day, this works.  We have friends who would need to upgrade the electrical service to the whole house to get 220V service to the garage, and they have gotten along fine with L1 charging. This works especially well since they have a second car. They also have a clothes dryer near the garage. They are able to use its 220V service or nearby Superchargers in special cases.

What if you live in a condo or other place where you can’t install your own charger? With considerable effort, my brother was able to get 220V service for L2 charging in the garage of his condo. If you have a L2 charger at work, it solves the problem. Also, there are many Superchargers in cities, and if you live near one, you can fill up there just as you would with a gas station.

The lowest price Tesla is the Model 3 RWD with seating for five, a range of 321 miles, at a price of $37,000 (see mine in the lead photo). That is plenty of range for both local and cross-country driving. However, if you want to up the range to 363 miles, you need to get the Model 3 Premium for $47,500. For only $3,000 more, you can get the base Model Y. It costs only $40,000. (See the photos below.) With the Model Y, you get a lot more room for baggage, you ride a little higher, you get a much bigger power liftgate, and you have the option of a factory-installed receiver/tow hitch, which is the one upgrade I chose because I often carry my ebikes on a rack in back. The standard Model Y seats five, but you can get the optional YL which seats 6 in three rows of captain chairs. I upgraded from my 2019 Model 3 to a base 2026 Model Y recently. You can get the premium Model Y with better external and internal styling and 363 miles of range for the RWD Model. It also comes with numerous other features, like faster charging, better audio speakers, and a small video screen for the rear seat passengers for another $5,000, as shown below. Four-wheel drive is a $2,000 option on all models and does reduce your range a little.

Every Tesla comes equipped with smart cruise control. Unfortunately, autosteer was eliminated recently unless you pay for Full Self Driving. If there is a car in front of you, you essentially have 100% self-driving until you need to make a turn. You have full automation in a traffic jam, where the car will move forward, automatically following the car in front of you.

I’m currently driving a base 2026 Model Y with FSD V14, which now costs $99/month. I can get into my car in my garage or a big parking lot or a Supercharger and without touching anything say, Hey Grok, then verbally say: Navigate me to [nearly any business, my home, or any address I dictate to the navigation]. Then FSD will drive me automatically out of my garage or the parking lot to the location requested and park my car in a parking lot or on the street (but not yet into my garage). I sit back and “supervise,” but seldom must intervene or complain about its actions as it drives.

“Tesla has introduced a new feature called Automatic Collision Evasion, which can autonomously activate Full Self-Driving (FSD) to avoid imminent collisions, even if the driver has not engaged the FSD system. This feature aims to enhance safety by allowing the vehicle to take evasive actions during critical situations.”

Note: In my opinion, Tesla customers that don’t opt in for the $99/month FSD subscription are missing a very important, possibly live saving safety feature.

There are literally 161 different new EV models to choose from in the US today. There are also several EV models that have been discontinued that you can buy used. If you can’t stomach buying a Tesla from Elon Musk’s company, you can choose one of those.

I will present two good alternatives to buying a Tesla: The top selling EVs in the US in 2026 to date right now are as follows. You can see that the Teslas are by far the most sold, but the Hyundai IONIQ 5 and the Toyota bz are reasonable alternatives for Musk haters.

Toyota led the movement towards automobiles that are more friendly to the environment with the Prius over 20 years ago. However, they were very slow to offer a compelling pure battery electric vehicle. That has now changed, Toyota’s bz EV has received excellent reviews. I had been a big customer of Toyota gas cars until recently, so I dropped down to the nearest Toyota dealership to see the status of EVs there, as shown in the photos below. #5787. Participation in Sunrun’s Puerto Rico VPP program doubled in 2026

Sunrun announced that participation in its Puerto Rico distributed power plant has doubled to more than 60,000 customers, representing nearly 70,000 home batteries. The distributed power plant frequently and reliably delivers 45 MW of dispatchable power for four hours at a time, providing critical support to Puerto Rico’s electric grid.

Since April, Sunrun has dispatched its power plant more than 60 times to weather severe energy shortfalls and prevent rolling blackouts. During an extended period of unseasonably hot temperatures this fall, Sunrun dispatched its home batteries to the grid for 18 consecutive days beginning October 13.

Sunrun’s Puerto Rico distributed power plant has scaled rapidly since 2024, when approximately 4,000 customers were enrolled. Enrollment surged to more than 30,000 customers in 2025. Sunrun is the largest aggregator in Puerto Rico’s Customer Battery Energy Sharing program.

Puerto Rico’s electric utility operator, LUMA, said in June and again in July that energy from home batteries helped “maintain system stability and prevent outages that would have affected thousands of customers.”

“What began in Puerto Rico out of necessity for family resilience has become a vital blueprint for the U.S. mainland, where severe weather and the proliferation of AI data centers are demanding new energy solutions,” said Sunrun CEO Mary Powell. “Sunrun is uniquely positioned to provide these solutions, whether to support a grid at its breaking point or to provide capacity for hyperscalers looking to come online.”

Sunrun’s increased dispatching capacity in Puerto Rico comes after residents on the island experienced an average of 36 hours of power outages in 2025 that were not caused by major adverse weather, which is 19% more than the previous year, according to the U.S. Energy Information Administration. When severe weather is factored in, residents experienced an additional 23 hours of power interruptions.

Customers who participate in Sunrun’s Puerto Rico distributed power plant choose how much energy to share, with most contributing a small portion of their total battery capacity. This approach ensures they retain reserve power for personal backup power in the event of grid outages. Participants are financially compensated for the power they choose to share, while Sunrun generates recurring revenue by operating the distributed power plant.

#5786. APS expects sales to double by 2041, plans extending coal use to 2038

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Peak demand will grow about 3.4% annually over the next 15 years, driven by industry and data centers, Arizona Public Service said in an integrated resource plan filed Friday with regulators.

APS projects peak demand of more than 14.5 GW in 2041, almost 60% higher than its current record peak of about 9.2 GW, set in August. And the utility expects annual retail sales to grow to about 80,600 GWh in 2041, up from about 34,200 GWh in 2025.

There is some uncertainty behind APS’ load projections, but the utility said it has taken steps to protect customers.

Despite refinements to APS’ large-load interconnection process, the “sustained record levels of interest” from large customers poses “a risk in increased potential for unrealized loads,” APS said in its resource plan, which it files every three years with the Arizona Corporation Commission. To mitigate the risk, APS said it adopted strategies such as having customers commit to “minimum demand and energy-based contract terms, providing collateral in case of default, and instituting early exit fees.”

As a result of population growth and higher levels of economic activity, APS said it expects to add up to 29,000 residential customers annually in the near term, and across the planning period the utility anticipates annual customer growth to be in the range of 1.5% to 2.5% through 2030.

The utility’s preferred plan includes continuing to operate the Four Corners coal plant beyond a planned 2031 retirement. APS said the extension could save customers close to $1 billion.

“The company will continue to evaluate the appropriate time to cease operations between 2031 and 2038, with the decision depending on customer needs, economics and other factors evaluated through future planning processes,” APS said in a statement.

“Reliability is nonnegotiable,” Mike Eugenis, APS director of resource planning, said in a statement.

APS’ planned natural gas resource additions include repowering the Cholla Power Plant as a 387-MW facility by 2030, adding 1,107 MW through new combustion turbines at existing APS sites and uprates to tolling units during 2026-2030, according to the IRP. Under the plan, APS will also add the 1,238 MW Desert Sun combined-cycle facility in 2032 to serve large-customer subscription load.

Customer and clean energy advocates were critical of the APS plan.

APS’ proposal “further delays our move from gas and coal to solar and wind,” Sierra Club Grand Canyon Chapter Director Sandy Bahr said in a statement. “We’re seeing these utilities protect their own bottom lines, instead of making the most economical decisions for ratepayers.”

The utility “should be investing in proven community-based energy solutions, like local solar and battery storage,” Vote Solar West Senior Regulatory Director Kate Bowman said in a statement.

APS “failed to consider how alternative technologies like rooftop solar, battery storage, and other distributed resources could help meet energy needs,” Bowman said. As the ACC considers the plan, regulators will have “an opportunity to push for a more comprehensive approach that puts affordability, reliability, and the interests of customers at the center of long-term planning,” she added.

The utility said it is adding nearly 2 GW of renewable utility-scale solar and 2.5 GW of battery storage in the next two years, “diversifying our resources to keep energy reliable and affordable.” By 2030, APS said it expects to have approximately 6 GW of battery storage on its system.

The utility also expects continued growth in customer-sited resources. APS said behind-the-meter storage adoption is expected to grow every year to 1.2 GW by 2045 — up from 69 MW today. For 2032, the storage forecast is approximately 95% higher compared to the last storage forecast developed in 2023, APS said.

Behind-the-meter solar adoption is expected to slow relative to the 2020-2023 period but “will still experience some modest growth,” reaching 3.7 GW by 2045 — up from 2.1 GW today.

Electric vehicles in APS service territory are expected to grow from 65,000 vehicles today to 1.1 million in 2045, resulting in 5.2 TWh of EV charging load, APS said. In 2042, this forecast is approximately 18% lower compared to the last EV forecast developed in 2023, it noted.

#5784. Exelon, Invenergy complaints at FERC seek capacity market changes

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Exelon asked the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission to order PJM to adopt a “prompt” capacity auction; Invenergy wants the Midcontinent grid operator to let HVDC lines receive capacity payments.

Exelon and Invenergy Transmission this week filed separate complaints at the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission seeking changes to the capacity markets run by the PJM Interconnection and the Midcontinent Independent System Operator.

Exelon asked FERC to order PJM to shift to a “prompt” auction from its current forward capacity auction schedule, according to a Tuesday filing at the agency. Currently, PJM holds annual base capacity auctions to secure power supplies three years in advance.

Under Exelon's proposal, PJM would hold its capacity auctions two months before the delivery year begins, starting with the 2030/31 delivery year. If FERC approves the proposal, PJM would hold its next capacity auction in December for the 2029/30 delivery year as planned, and then it would hold its next auction in early 2030.

Pushing out the date of the 2030/31 auction would give PJM time to overhaul its capacity market, which Exelon contends is no longer working properly, according to the complaint.

Adopting a prompt auction would help ensure that auctions are based on more accurate load forecasts, resource availability data and capacity accreditation levels, Exelon said. It would also facilitate more efficient resource entry and exit through improved market signals, the Chicago-based utility company said. Exelon’s utilities have about 10.7 million customers in PJM’s Mid-Atlantic and Midwest footprint.

Exelon asked FERC to make a decision on the complaint by Jan. 31, the last day before PJM is set to release the planning parameters for the 2030/31 delivery year base auction, which is set to be held in May.

PJM is reviewing the complaint and will reply at “the appropriate time,” Jeffrey Shields, a PJM spokesman, said.

The grid operator is accepting proposals from stakeholders on how it could reform its capacity market through its Power Reliability Through Market Design initiative, Shields noted. PJM has received more than 30 responses to its request for papers on the issue. They will be discussed at a Nov. 13 workshop.

When it kicked off the effort in May, PJM proposed three broad reform pathways: stabilizing its existing capacity market, rationing grid reliability among customer classes or regions and shifting to a market focused on energy and ancillary services and away from the capacity market.

Meanwhile, Invenergy Transmission on Wednesday told FERC that MISO improperly bars high-voltage, direct-current merchant transmission lines from its capacity market.

MISO counts the capacity value of HVDC lines when it decides how much capacity it needs to buy in its capacity auctions, a move that reduces the amount of capacity it acquires, according to the complaint.

But, MISO’s rules prevent those lines from being accredited as capacity resources or their owners otherwise being paid for their capacity value, allowing the grid operator to benefit from the lines for free, Invenergy said.

“MISO cannot rely on that capacity, to the benefit of other customers, while refusing to provide any avenue for the entity that provides that capacity to receive commensurate compensation,” the Chicago-based company said.

Instead of recovering their costs through FERC-approved cost-of-service rates, the owners of merchant lines recover their costs via rates negotiated with their customers, Invenergy noted.

“Interregional merchant HVDC transmission lines only receive compensation if there is a product or service — such as capacity — for which they can transact with potential customers,” Invenergy said.

Without capacity payments from MISO, “there is no product or service that can be the basis of a merchant-based transaction in MISO,” Invenergy said.

Using a “direct loss of load” accreditation methodology, Invenergy’s Grain Belt Express HVDC transmission line could provide more than 1,800 MW of accredited capacity, according to the complaint.

The 5-GW, 600-kV Grain Belt project is slated to run about 800 miles from Kansas to Indiana. Invenergy plans to build the roughly $11 billion project in two phases, with the first phase running from southwest Kansas to central Missouri.

#5783. XPENG Researchers and Academic Partners Introduce GroundingPI, An Open-Source Visual Perception Model For Physical AI

Guangzhou, China — XPENG (NYSE: XPEV, HKEX: 9868), a leading Physical AI company, has supported the development of GroundingPI, an open-source visual perception foundation model created by researchers affiliated with XPENG, Peking University, The University of Hong Kong and other academic institutions.

The research explores a new approach to embodied intelligence by focusing on a fundamental capability: enabling AI systems to accurately identify objects and understand their spatial positions in the physical world. With approximately 4 billion parameters, GroundingPI achieved the highest aggregate score among 44 baseline models evaluated across 34 perception benchmarks covering 11 capability categories.

The research paper, model weights and supporting code have been made publicly available, enabling researchers and developers to further explore perception-focused approaches to Physical AI.

As AI increasingly moves beyond digital environments into physical applications such as robotics and autonomous driving, accurately understanding the surrounding environment becomes essential.

While general-purpose foundation models have demonstrated strong capabilities in language understanding, reasoning and planning, physical intelligence requires more precise visual perception. AI systems must not only recognize objects but also identify their exact locations and spatial relationships to support real-world interaction.

GroundingPI takes a perception-focused approach to this challenge. Rather than relying on robot action labels during the perceptual model’s training, the research focuses on developing foundational visual understanding capabilities that can support downstream action-learning systems.

For example, when instructed to identify “the handle of the cup on the right”, an AI system needs to locate not only the correct cup but also the precise position of its handle. GroundingPI is designed to represent such information using spatial coordinates, including points and bounding boxes.

The model is trained across 11 categories of perceptual capabilities, including object detection, dense object localization, referring expression grounding, optical character recognition (OCR), document layout understanding, user interface element recognition and robotic spatial localization.

By strengthening these fundamental capabilities, the research explores how specialized perception models could provide a more effective visual foundation for embodied AI systems.

In evaluations conducted by the research team, GroundingPI was compared with 44 baseline models across 34 public perception benchmarks spanning 11 capability categories.

Using an aggregate evaluation score with equal weighting across the capability categories, GroundingPI achieved 73.68, compared with 71.54 for GPT-6 Astra.

The results demonstrate the potential of specialized perception-focused models to outperform larger general-purpose models on specific visual grounding tasks. The comparison relates to the evaluated perception benchmarks and does not represent a broader assessment of general AI intelligence or reasoning capabilities.

GroundingPI’s performance was particularly relevant to tasks requiring precise object localization, including complex visual scenes containing numerous objects or small targets.

These findings suggest that targeted perceptual pretraining can provide a valuable foundation for future Physical AI research, complementing the broader reasoning and planning capabilities of general-purpose foundation models.

Beyond perception benchmarks, the research team evaluated GroundingPI as a visual foundation for downstream robotic manipulation and autonomous driving tasks.

The results highlight two potential advantages for embodied intelligence.

Robotic systems often require extensive human demonstrations to learn new manipulation tasks, creating significant challenges in data collection and training.

In RoboCasa-GR1 simulation experiments, robotic learning systems using GroundingPI as their visual backbone achieved higher task success rates with 50% of the available demonstration data than the evaluated comparison models trained with 75%.

This finding suggests that stronger foundational perception capabilities may reduce the amount of action-training data required for certain robotic tasks, potentially improving the efficiency of future embodied AI development.

Another major challenge in robotics is generalization: the ability to perform a learned task when objects, backgrounds or environmental conditions change.

In RoboTwin 2.0 simulation evaluations, GroundingPI demonstrated stronger performance than the evaluated mainstream visual backbones across four out-of-distribution settings, indicating improved adaptability to unfamiliar environments.

The research team also evaluated its potential for autonomous driving perception using the nuScenes dataset. When integrated as a visual backbone, GroundingPI achieved an average open-loop trajectory prediction error of 0.296 metres in the reported evaluation.

Together, these experimental findings highlight the potential of perception-focused foundation models to support more adaptable and data-efficient embodied intelligence systems.

The research remains exploratory. Its downstream results are based on simulation and offline evaluations and do not establish readiness for real-world commercial deployment.

GroundingPI explores a research direction in which specialized visual perception capabilities complement higher-level reasoning and planning, rather than relying exclusively on general-purpose models for every component of an embodied intelligence system.

The findings provide insights into how perception-focused pretraining could contribute to future research on robotic manipulation, autonomous driving and other applications requiring precise interaction with the physical world.

To encourage further exploration and collaboration, the research team has made GroundingPI’s paper, model weights and supporting code publicly accessible, subject to the applicable open-source and third-party licensing terms.

The project represents an academic research contribution supported by XPENG and its research collaborators. The results do not constitute an announcement of integration into XPENG’s commercial products or a commitment to future deployment.

Project Website: https://groundingpi.github.io/ Research Paper: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39601 Source Code: https://github.com/groundingpi/GroundingPI Model Weights: https://huggingface.co/GroundingPI/GroundingPI

XPENG is a leading global Physical AI company, dedicated to bringing artificial intelligence into the physical world to reshape future mobility and smart living. Through in-house R&D, XPENG has developed a full-stack Physical AI architecture spanning Turing AI chips, world foundation models, and highly integrated software and hardware applications. This unified technology foundation of XPENG powers an expansive product portfolio of smart EVs, robotaxis, and humanoid robots, advancing the deployment of Physical AI at scale.

Headquartered in Guangzhou, China, XPENG is dual-primary listed on the New York Stock Exchange and the Hong Kong Stock Exchange. With global capabilities across R&D, manufacturing, sales, and services, XPENG drives continuous technological innovation and fosters an open Physical AI ecosystem, making life smarter, safer, and better for users worldwide. For more information, please visit https://www.xpeng.com/.

#5780. Entergy sees progress on Arkansas data center deal, strong Q3 sales

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The New Orleans-based company separately inked firm pipeline contracts for enough natural gas to support 10 new combined-cycle power plants in Louisiana, CEO Drew Marsh said Monday.

Entergy’s amended application to serve a controversial Google data center will save its Arkansas ratepayers at least $500 million more than the $1.6 billion previously agreed, CEO Drew Marsh told investors Monday during the company’s third-quarter earnings call.

The New Orleans-based utility holding company saw earnings increase more than 20% over the year-ago period helped by warmer-than-average weather across its four-state U.S. territory in the Midcontinent Independent System Operator’s southern zones, he said. Total electricity sales rose 5% year over year.

Entergy made progress on two key regulatory priorities in Louisiana, which accounts for nearly half of the holding company’s retail sales.

State regulators in October approved Entergy Louisiana’s $1.2 billion, 500-kV Babel to Webre transmission project, Marsh said. And Louisiana Public Service Commission staff separately reached a settlement with the utility that Entergy said “resolves all outstanding issues” between the company, LPSC staff and one intervenor in a contested proceeding related to several gigawatts of generation and related infrastructure to serve Meta’s planned Hyperion data center campus.

The LPSC is set to vote on the matter next month, Entergy said.

Meanwhile, Entergy’s Mississippi utility asked state regulators last month for state clearance to issue bonds that would offset approximately $237.8 million of costs related to Winter Storm Fern, which Entergy said caused “severe damage” to its infrastructure.

And in Texas, Entergy said it has energized the Orange County Advanced Power Station, a 1,215-MW combined-cycle gas plant built to support load growth in the state’s southeastern corner.

Entergy filed the amended Google service contract with Arkansas regulators last month. If approved, Entergy would add 350 MW of battery energy storage at two sites and procure 440 MW of solar from Pattern Energy’s nearby Big Island Solar project, the company said in a required securities filing this week.

The Arkansas Public Service Commission is set to hear the matter in January, Entergy said.

The amended contract would expand power demand from Google’s West Memphis data center, but the exact amount and other key details are redacted, the Arkansas Democrat-Gazette reported on Oct 20.

On Monday, Marsh said Google would pay $600 million toward the cost of the battery systems and suggested the tech giant would avoid passing costs to other Entergy ratepayers.

“This agreement ensures that Google will cover its fair share, which includes the cost of the new investments as well as their share of system costs,” Marsh said.

Entergy Arkansas is one of five utility companies owned by Entergy, and earned about 48% of its sales from industrial customers in 2025. The parent company’s four-state territory has been a popular destination for large-scale data centers like Google’s Arkansas project and Meta’s even larger Louisiana campus.

Together, data center and traditional industrial customers account for a large share of Entergy’s expected future sales growth, much of which is not yet reflected in the company’s near-term capital plan, Marsh said.

“We have a strong pipeline of projects in varying stages that are not in our base plan,” he said. “Data center interest remains strong and in the range of 7 [GW] to 12 GW. Our traditional industrial segments continue with discussions for 3 [GW] to 5 GW.”

Those large-load pipeline figures are largely unchanged from recent quarters’ projections. But the company’s projected four-year capital spending has risen significantly over the past year, to about $55 billion as of Sept. 30.

Marsh said the Google contract allows Entergy to keep base rates flat for Arkansas customers. However, that’s not the case in Louisiana, where Meta “is helping [to] offset some [infrastructure] costs, but not necessarily to make it go back to flat like we were in Arkansas,” he said.

To serve the Meta data center, Entergy plans to spend about $12.9 billion to build seven combined-cycle gas-fired power plants with 5,278 MW of combined capacity, plus a $1.4 billion transmission line and 200 MW of battery storage. It has also asked Louisiana regulators to approve a corporate sustainability agreement that would see Meta — doing business as Evest LLC — cover the cost of 2,500 MW of new renewables and energy storage resources.

To support the new gas generation, Entergy inked firm transportation agreements with “large midstream and pipeline companies” for 1.325 billion cubic feet/day of natural gas, Marsh said.

“That's enough for Entergy to supply fuel to 10 new combined cycles in Louisiana … and also helps build new pipeline capacity that benefits our industrial customers' growth,” he said.

#5778. PowerBank completes 3-MW New York community solar project

Renewable energy developer PowerBank has completed the 3.26-MW Camillus community solar project in Camillus, New York, and has received permission to operate. Solar Advocate Development, LLC, the project’s owner and EPC, built the array on a capped landfill, which it acquired from PowerBank in spring 2025 in a $7.3 million transaction.

“A closed landfill is land that most people would write off,” said Andrew van Doorn, president and COO of PowerBank. “At Camillus, our engineers and construction partners turned it into a 3.26-MW source of clean power for local households. #5777. Thermal Dawn — Thermal Storage Is Cheap Storage

Another great idea from Down Under: Thermal Dawn promises cost effective heating and cooling, mainly by replacing gas boilers in cold climates. CleanTechnica had a chance to interview Nick Zeniou, Founder of Thermal Dawn, recently by phone, fresh after his presentation at Everything Electric. You can hear him explain his revolutionary system here, or here with a little less drama.

Everything Electric was a good opportunity to measure public reaction to the new product. Nick tells me that hundreds of people checked out their stall. “People care about carbon cost and comfort. People don’t compromise on comfort.” Sounds like comfort trumps carbon cost. Homeowners who visited the stand and filled out the survey were focused on sustainability and cost. Nick cautions: “it was an electrification expo crowd, and 50 of the 62 already had solar, so it’s not the whole market.” See the chart below.

Although I live in the warmer part of Australia (Brisbane), many Aussies have to endure cold winters. Nick tells me he is looking at installs in Sydney, Canberra, and Melbourne in the deep south. Thermal Dawn can store heat up to 70 degrees Celsius, or down to 5 degrees. Thermal Dawns’ equipment can run cold or hot water through pipes and radiators that are already installed, depending on the need — without having to run an expensive gas boiler. He describes it as a thermal battery. Just to be clear, it does not generate electricity.

Check out this graph below, which shows the Thermal Dawn system in action at a house in Melbourne during a day in August 2026. It shows room temperature and when the heat pump was running, in 15-minute samples, unedited. The heat pump ran until about 1:45pm, then not again, heated by stored energy through the evening peak.

“What about return on investment,” I asked. He described two systems they have had running over the southern Australian winter which have saved the homeowners approximately AU$1,500 each. “The first was a house in Melbourne, our first install, live since May 2026. The owners charge the system in their retailer’s free three-hour midday window, so the heat for the evening costs them close to nothing. The second was a Warragul (slab-heated home, Victoria): installed in two days in July 2026. Our controls turn the home’s concrete slab into part of the battery, managing when it charges and when it releases heat. On 95% of winter evenings, the heat pumps did not run at all between 5 pm and 9pm. Since commissioning, 70,521 MJ of gas has been avoided by just these two installations.”

Nick further explains: “A standard heat pump draws from the grid during those expensive morning and evening hours. You’ve escaped gas, but you’re still paying premium rates every time the temperature drops at night. Thermal Dawn is different. Our system charges when power is cheapest, a free midday window, your own solar, or late-night off-peak, then delivers that stored warmth through your radiators and underfloor heating all evening, with the compressor off through the expensive hours.

It appears that Thermal Dawn’s secret sauce is “Phase Change Materials.” Naturally, Nick declined to share exactly what Thermal Dawn is using. The system replaces a gas boiler with a combination of reverse cycle heat pump, well insulated water tank, and phase change materials. PCMs are designed to freeze or melt at a specific temperature, storing and releasing heat when they change physical state, usually from solid to liquid. “PCM doubles our heat capacity.” The heating applications I could understand, what about the cooling? “Cool water from the tank runs through a fan coil unit, and the fan blows cool air into the room. Cooling is an optional add-on.” That’s the answer.

How do thermal storage and a home battery/solar system work together? “Thermal storage is cheap storage doing one job very well. Heating, cooling, and hot water are the largest, most predictable loads in the house, and covering them thermally is far cheaper per kWh than covering them from lithium. A battery is expensive storage doing a general-purpose job.

“The practical effect is that your battery stops being the thing that carries your heating. It’s freed up for lights, appliances, the EV, and overnight backup, and it lasts longer for not being cycled hard every winter evening. Homes with a battery already are often the clearest case, because the expensive storage stops being spent on the cheapest possible job.”

Thermal dawn connects to existing hydronic pipework. The gas boiler with its high gas bills is gone. Check out the animation of heat discharge overnight. “The thermal battery charged on solar during the day and now heats the house while the heat pump stays off. It’s an illustration of what the app shows, not a recording of a live home.” For those rooms that don’t have hydronic radiators or underfloor slab heating, Thermal Dawn installs fan coil units. Nick tells me via a followup email that: “It’s a small indoor unit with a water coil and a fan: warm water from the thermal battery runs through the coil, and the fan blows the heating or cooling into the room.”

The heavy technical discussion out of the way, I asked Nick about his background. He grew up in an isolated part of New Zealand. His job was to collect the firewood for the family’s heat source — a fireplace. He had to get the fire going by 4:00 pm or the house would not warm up for the cold evening. Timing was very important, as it is for electricity use.

Nick is a mechanical engineer who has worked across tech companies and startups. While working on installing commercial air conditioning, he came across the idea that became Thermal Dawn. Nick joined the dots between the huge variations in electricity supply cost throughout each 24-hour period, with an average home’s thermal energy needs (up to two-thirds of consumption) and the fact that homes are becoming power stations. Thence to thermal batteries powered by Thermal Dawn technology.

He reminisces about the start of the business: “When our first unit was finished, we loaded it into a ute at 5pm and I drove it down the Hume overnight, Sydney to Melbourne (1000 km), to install it the next morning. We had a four-month-old at home at the time. My wife Linda is my co-founder and leads mechanical design and manufacturing, so while I was on the road, she was looking after a newborn.”

For those who would like to get involved, Nick invites Australian homeowners to sign up here and they’ll be in touch. “Readers overseas: send us a message or request a quote at the same link to register interest. If we hear from enough people in one area, we can bring forward our plans to come there.”

Having proven their point, Thermal Dawn is cleared to sell and installing its first batch now, with a larger product for ducted air conditioning in development. I asked about apartment block heating and Nick confirmed that they are looking at it but want to get the domestic home sorted first.

I asked a couple of followup questions “What about the Victorian government’s position?” Nick’s answer:  “Victoria is set to bring in support for systems like ours from the middle of next year. With the state election in November, there’s talk of a change in direction and of belt-tightening, so we’ve built Thermal Dawn to stand on its own. It saves households money without a subsidy, and any support is a bonus.”

Any plans for installs in Canberra? “Yes. Canberra is one of the best places in Australia for this: cold winters, plenty of hydronic homes, and a territory already moving off gas. We’re talking with installers there now.”

I really appreciate these technological experts when they take the time to explain things to me. Sometimes I feel like Winnie the Pooh — a bear of very little brain. Looks like Thermal Dawn has solved another part of the energy puzzle — good news for homeowners and also for the planet.

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

#5776. Co-located solar, storage jumps to 16% of all solar installations, up from 2% in 2017: EIA

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The share of solar projects co-located with storage is projected to pass 30% over the next two years, based on planned additions, according to a report from the U.S. Energy Information Administration.

The share of new solar projects deployed with co-located battery storage this year is 16% — or about 41 GW — as of September, and may reach 19% by year’s end, said an Oct. 30 report from the U.S. Energy Information Administration. It is likely to pass 30% in the next two years, the agency estimated.

The trend has increased significantly over the last decade, as only around 2% of projects, or 5 GW, of new solar capacity was co-located with storage in 2017. In 2023, co-location jumped to 25% of projects, then fell over 2024 and 2025, according to EIA’s data — but is projected to pass 30% over the next two years, based on planned additions.

“When operators co-locate battery storage with solar, they can direct solar electricity output to the grid or to the battery, depending on the wholesale prices,” EIA’s report said. “Wholesale prices in regions with large solar participation tend to fall when solar contributions are high and rise as they decrease.”

“Plants with both solar and battery storage can manage the combined output of their generating resources to maximize their value in a changing wholesale price environment,” EIA added.

An Oct. 22 report from the Solar Energy Industries Association found that from 2021 to 2026, “solar generation increased 421% in Texas and 90% in California, while battery storage capacity grew more than 26-fold in Texas and nearly sevenfold in California.”

When comparing the same June to August period, SEIA found that “CAISO used 2.5% more power than the same time in 2021 with an average wholesale electricity price 40% lower after accounting for inflation. ERCOT used 30.1% more power than the same time in 2021 with an average wholesale price 42% lower after accounting for inflation.”

EIA’s report noted that Southwest states “have higher-than-average solar capacity shares relative to their total generating capacity and also have higher levels of co-located utility-scale solar and battery capacity.”

“As of September, 13% of the total U.S. utility-scale generating capacity is solar photovoltaic, and 18% of that is co-located with battery storage,” EIA said. “The share of solar generation co-located with battery capacity in California, Arizona, New Mexico, and Nevada is more than double the national average.”

A September analysis from Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory found that at the end of 2025, there were 436 co-located solar-plus-storage projects in the United States, with a total of 29 GW of solar capacity and close to 17 GW of storage capacity.

Within the interconnection queue, LBNL found that solar-plus-storage facilities comprise 86% of proposed hybrid facilities, at close to 361 GW of planned capacity.

#5773. How Can Europe Accelerate Electric Trucking?

Europe is having a hard time lately, with skyrocketing oil prices dampening economies. Fuel costs, particularly diesel, are prompting surging interest in truck electrification. Europe has been urging truck makers to move toward electric trucking, and they have responded with a variety of electric semi trucks from many manufacturers. Europe is a group of sovereign countries with many standards. The European Union is a political and economic union of 27 states in that area. It issued directives urging action and imposing penalties. In response to European Union directives, companies have formed EV100, a coalition of corporations responding to the need. In a recent story, Steve Hanley wrote about European electric truck makers’ difficulties expanding truck electrification. Here’s a quotation from the story: “In a letter to EU Commission president Ursula von der Leyen, of primary concern, in their estimation, is expanding the charging infrastructure for heavy-duty trucks in Europe.” There is no shortage of electric trucks in Europe. Expansion could go faster were it not for charging infrastructure. Manufacturers complain about “an alarming delay” in conditions needed to drive market uptake. For Europe, truck electrification is not working as well as in China, where over 90% of the world’s electric trucks reside. The differences between China and the EU show some reasons why truck electrification is slower in Europe. How did China succeed where the EU failed? China made electric trucks a freight system, not a vehicle category.

Power is a major factor in electric grid infrastructure. Charging electric trucks places demands on power. Peak power determines how much infrastructure is necessary. In grid planning, peak annual power determines how much capital equipment is necessary. Reducing peak power demand is a critical factor for electric truck infrastructure. Figure 1 shows one method of reducing power called route stuffing. It schedules departures on heavy traffic corridors at regularly spaced intervals. Corridors like ones between ports and warehouses are a target for those practices. These corridors often route trucks in a round trip chain. Filling routes with scheduled trips can reduce power demand compared to random trip scheduling. The article Battery Swap Uses Fewer Batteries Than You Think is devoted to methods of analysis and planning that can lead to lower peak power demand. There are other benefits to planning, such as greater truck load capacity from the same amount of batteries. Part of the solution to slow electric truck infrastructure development is to plan infrastructure and truck use practices to reduce the charging power burden. Given the pressing and enduring need due to the diesel fuel cost crisis, it is necessary to reprioritize grid power upgrade demands. Demands for data center power upgrades like transformers for substations should be re shuffled and prioritized toward the diesel crisis.

In two articles, Michael Barnard outlined the issues. Vehicles are not a transportation system. What does Europe have now? It has an EU Commission interacting with a coalition, EV100. The problem is, there are plenty of trucks available, but who is in charge of infrastructure? You can’t have electric trucks without means of charging, but as the coalition complains, “Significant improvements to charging infrastructure, a reduction in carbon dioxide based tolls, and a coherent policy framework are urgently needed.” There are so many stakeholders — including the commission, vehicles makers, and charging companies across multiple countries — that no one is in charge of infrastructure, and without it, nothing moves. It needs a unity of purpose and action.

If China is succeeding, why not adopt its strategy? Perhaps the answer is right in front of everyone’s nose. As Michael puts it, “the policy connects vehicles to corridors, depots, service areas, grids and freight use cases instead of treating sales targets as sufficient.” CATL offered Europe a system, not a vehicle. This is a “turn key” solution. It’s a well thought out system, including use of methods and practices, not an anything goes free-for-all. That kind of stability and predictability is what EV100 leaders are looking for. This is not a swap-or-nothing scenario. Different techniques apply to various use cases. Swap must be planned for high-use freight corridors and other proper applications. Swap trucks can also fast charge or slow charge, providing flexibility and a system of trucks that includes smaller trucks, not just semis.

What does all this have to do with Europe’s slowly advancing infrastructure? Simply put, electric trucks put heavy charging demands on the grid. Trucks can have megawatt-hour batteries that require megawatt power charging to charge in an hour. Reducing grid power demands can reduce grid infrastructure upgrade costs, and leverage capital investment toward faster implementation.

“The process of getting megawatt charging equipment connected to the grid can take several years, delaying deployment even where operators and infrastructure providers are ready to invest.” The need for megawatt charging equipment is reduced by accelerating swap network development instead of fixed battery trucks that must be charged by fast chargers. “Swaptopus is easy to misread as a quirky name for another electric-truck infrastructure project. It is a newly formed joint venture between Octopus Energy and CATL, created to develop automated battery-swapping hubs for heavy trucks across Europe.” Unlike the government of China that played a role in encouraging standards and cooperation in infrastructure, Europe has a looser arrangement between many countries with various standards. Europe is learning the slow, hard way where battery swapping is most needed.

Every truck application has different optimization. It means that there must be different approaches to fit freight operations in heavy traffic corridors. Quoting Michael Barnard, “Freight operators often run repeated routes between ports, factories, distribution centres and logistics hubs. Those patterns provide known traffic, known stopping points and relatively predictable electricity demand. A swap hub can be placed around an existing freight flow instead of hoping that enough unrelated trucks eventually pass through.”

Freight corridors are a scenario made to order for battery swap. Real trucking solutions include a mix of vehicle and infrastructure types — from small, local delivery, to large, long-haul freight; and from slow, overnight charging, to medium power charging and battery swap — all used in concert to accelerate heavy truck electrification. With a wide variety of charging options, and vehicles and charging use cases targeted in coordination to avoid peak power demands, the path forward is eased. School buses don’t need expensive high-power fast charging. Lower-power chargers can do the trick. Local delivery can use a mix of overnight and medium power charging. Heavy truck dispatch must be more carefully coordinated to avoid peak demand and grid upgrade costs. A mix of medium-power charging and swap stations along with planned use cases can successfully lower electric freight power needs and transport system capital expenses to speed transport electrification.

Transformation and conversion of diesel freight systems to electric requires coordination and planning between government agencies and coalitions of corporations, cities, government agencies, and planners. Transportation is a system basic to societies and economics. Coordination between groups at all levels results in practices, use cases, and infrastructure that produce benefits for all. Grid expansion and construction can take years. In order for Europe to implement truck freight electrification, more structure and planning is needed to minimize grid power requirements, speed implementation, and maximize capital investment results. Transportation system, practices, and infrastructure must be planned for optimum results.

has studied wind, electric vehicles, and environmental issues. An electrical engineer familiar with power and electronics, he has participated in the Automotive X Prize contest. He is an avid writer, specializing in electric vehicles, batteries, and wind energy.

#5771. Eaton CEO: AC to DC migration for data centers ‘is real’

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The electrical equipment giant says it’s poised to capitalize on the trend by investing in solid-state transformers. But the “timing of that transition is not under our control,” said CEO Paul Ruiz.

The CEO of one of the world’s biggest suppliers of electrical equipment sees the AI-driven global construction boom continuing for years — and driving a shift from traditional low-voltage systems to 800-volt direct-current architecture for data centers.

In August 2025, Eaton Corp. paid $86 million for Resilient Power Systems, a solid-state transformer developer.

Using magnets and semiconductors rather than specialized steel alloys and vast amounts of copper wiring, solid-state transformers, or SSTs, are more compact and efficient than traditional low- and medium-voltage transformers. Data centers built on the 800-volt direct-current architecture that Eaton expects will soon become the industry standard could reduce non-IT capital expenditures by up to 18%, according to a recent McKinsey analysis.

Earlier this year, the Electric Power Research Institute, New York Power Authority and North Carolina State University tested a 1-MW SST under “real-world” conditions and cut power losses in half compared to a traditional transformer configuration.

Paulo Ruiz, CEO of Eaton, told investors on Tuesday that the company’s 15-kV, 2-MW SST became, “to our knowledge,” the first multi-megawatt unit to pass all tests under a new UL Solutions standard for the technology.

However, it may be some time before Eaton SSTs are widely deployed, he cautioned.

“The good thing for us to keep in mind is that this direction of travel, in terms of migrating from AC to DC, is real because of the large power savings that the customers see in this new technology,” he said. “But the timing of that transition is not under our control.”

He added that SSTs are only one of several “key building blocks” needed, along with DC breaker, power quality and advanced cooling technologies.

“The AC architectures are not bad, and we are fine either way,” Ruiz said. “We win in either path.”

Ruiz said he sees no letup in the global demand for power equipment from data centers and utilities.

Eaton, which is headquartered in Ireland but is one of the largest suppliers in the U.S. market, makes and markets low- and medium-voltage electrical products such as single- and three-phase power quality systems, utility-grade power distribution equipment, circuit protection and power reliability equipment. It reported a record $8.9 billion in third-quarter sales, up 28% year over year.

“We track the [worldwide] projects that are announced, and now they amount to 366 GW,”  Ruiz said, of data centers. “Contrast that with the installed capacity of 50 GW. It's an enormous build-out. In my opinion, this will last for many years to come.”

Eaton predicts end market growth of about 11% in 2027. In 2025, data centers and distributed IT accounted for 21% of sales, while utilities accounted for 11%.

A little less than half the total of third-quarter sales came from Eaton’s Electrical Americas segment. That unit benefits from broad-based demand from data centers and other electrification “mega-projects,” helping to widen profit margins, Ruiz said.

The Electrical Americas segment saw its backlog rise to $16 billion – up 33% year over year –  while orders increased 39% on a rolling 12-month basis.

Ruiz emphasized that data centers were not Eaton’s only high-growth customer segment. Utility investment and other large-load industrial customers are propelling business, too, he said.

“Demand continues to run ahead of what we can convert,” Ruiz said. “This is a business that continues to benefit from powerful secular trends, utility investment, grid modernization, industrial reshoring, AI-driven data center expansion and broader electrification.”

In the past two years, Eaton has acquired multiple companies with data center exposure.

The most recent was a roughly $920 million deal for COL Group, an Italian manufacturer of medium-voltage electrical distribution equipment, grid automation technologies and modular power systems that Eaton said would boost its sales to data center and utility customers in Europe.

In March, Eaton paid about $9.6 billion to acquire Boyd Thermal, “a U.S.-based global leader in thermal components, systems, and ruggedized solutions for data center, aerospace and other end-markets,” the company said in a securities filing this week.

The Boyd pickup will help Eaton develop integrated power and cooling architectures for data center customers and “deliver differentiated grid-to-chip solutions while increasing our exposure to some of the fastest-growing segments of the data center market,” Ruiz said.

#5770. ARRAY Technologies and Shoals plan to merge companies

Solar tracker and racking manufacturer ARRAY Technologies and electrical balance of systems (eBOS) provider Shoals Technologies have initiated a stock-for-stock merger to combine both companies.

“This transaction represents a compelling opportunity to create meaningful value for customers of both ARRAY and Shoals by bringing together our complementary strengths. Although structural and electrical systems must operate seamlessly together in the field, they have historically been designed and sourced through separate channels. This combination changes that. Together, we expect to deliver a differentiated, technically integrated and interoperable platform that simplifies project execution, enhances project economics and accelerates innovation for customers,” said Kevin Hostetler, CEO of ARRAY.

According to the press announcement, ARRAY will be Shoals’ parent company after the transaction, with Shoal’s CEO Brandon Moss assuming the role as CEO for the overall company. ARRAY’s CEO Kevin Hostetler will transition to a special advisor role for the company.

“Today marks an important milestone for Shoals, ARRAY and the customers we serve. By bringing together two highly complementary businesses with experienced teams, we are creating an integrated platform with broader capabilities, deeper customer relationships and a stronger foundation for long-term growth. We believe the combined company will be positioned to better service customers, deliver more innovative and integrated solutions, realize meaningful synergies and pursue expansion opportunities across the solar value chain, data center markets and beyond,” said Brandon Moss, CEO of Shoals.

The companies plan to create packaged warranties across their structural and eBOS products. As manufacturers, both ARRAY and Shoals have supplied their products to solar and energy storage projects globally.

ARRAY is known for producing single-axis solar trackers that use an inter-row rotating mechanism. Over the years, the company has iterated on its trackers to use multi- and dual-row architectures, and to be deployed on undulating topographies. These rotating solar structures are supported with proprietary tracker operations software with considerations such as shading and hail and wind stowing.

Shoals manufactures combiner boxes and cable management solutions for multi-market scale solar and energy storage projects, as well as data centers.

“Having had the privilege of serving as chairman of both ARRAY and Shoals, I have seen firsthand the complementary products, talented teams and shared commitment to customers offered by both companies,” said Brad Forth, chairman of the board of directors of ARRAY and Shoals. “Together, ARRAY and Shoals are well positioned to deliver enhanced value to customers, shareholders and employees. I also want to thank Kevin for his leadership and important contributions to ARRAY, as well as his valuable insights as he transitions to a Special Advisor role. I look forward to continuing to work with Brandon, who brings proven leadership, deep industry expertise and strategic vision to lead the combined company in this next phase of growth.”

Both ARRAY’s and Shoals’ boards have reportedly unanimously approved the merger, with Forth abstaining from the vote. The merger is expected to complete in Q1 2027.

While not mergers, ARRAY Technologies has been acquiring companies producing technologies complementary to solar trackers. Last year, ARRAY acquired fixed-tilt racking and foundations manufacturer APA Solar Racking, and just acquired Affordable Wire Management, a cable management solutions provider, this past September.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5768. DTE expands investment plan 30%, will sell unregulated subsidiary to fund growth

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

After the $3 billion sale of DTE Vantage, DTE “becomes a higher growth premium utility” and “a simpler business,” CEO Joi Harris told analysts Thursday.

DTE Energy has agreed to sell its non-utility subsidiary for $3 billion, allowing the company to sharpen its focus on its regulated electric utility and fund a growing investment pipeline, company officials said Thursday.

DTE serves 2.3 million customers in southeastern Michigan, including Detroit.

DTE Vantage builds and operates energy projects including renewable natural gas. The sale to institutional investors is expected to close in the first half of 2027, DTE President and CEO Joi Harris said on the company’s third-quarter earnings call.

Data center growth is providing “once-in-a-generation opportunities” and “real affordability benefits to our customers,” Harris told analysts.

“Selling Vantage just allows us to sharpen our focus on the regulated utility, and it helps us execute on those investments, and it reduces clearly our equity and debt needs to fund them,” she said.

After the sale of Vantage, DTE Energy “becomes a higher growth premium utility” with long-term operating earnings per share growth of more than 9% annually after 2027, and “a simpler business construct,” Harris noted.

DTE’s new 5-year investment plan, spanning 2027-2031, now stands at $44.2 billion, including $39 billion at its DTE Electric subsidiary and $5.2 billion in its gas operations. The 2026-2030 investment plans totaled $30 billion and $4.5 billion, respectively.

On the electric side, the company’s spending plan includes $23 billion for cleaner generation and $12 billion in distribution investments to harden DTE’s grid system and improve reliability. Generation investments include 7.6 GW of solar and wind additions over the 5-year plan, 2.1 GW of natural gas and 2.7 GW of energy storage.

DTE received approval from Michigan regulators in October to supply a 1-GW Google data center, and last year finalized a 1.4-GW deal with Oracle.

“These projects will provide unprecedented customer bill benefits by spreading system fixed costs across a larger base of electric sales to help keep bills more affordable for existing customers,” Harris said. “The timing of these customer load ramps also creates an opportunity to delay our next electric rate case filing.”

DTE filed a $474 million electric rate case with the Michigan Public Service Commission in April. If the utility receives regulatory approval, Harris said the company won’t need to request new rates again until 2029.

Harris said the company is “continuing to have discussions” with regulators about multiyear rate making “in anticipation” of the state legislature taking that up toward the end of 2027.

“We have a strong and have maintained a strong relationship with both sides of the aisles over our history, and we are expecting nothing different with this cycle,” she said following a national surge for Democrats in the midterm elections, driven partly by backlash to data centers and energy costs.

DTE continues to see “strong demand” for new large-load development, Harris noted. Beyond the Oracle and Google data center deals, the utility currently has a pipeline of 5-6 GW of additional opportunities, including roughly 2 GW in advanced discussions, she noted.

“We are encouraged by the continued level of customer interest and remain focused on securing an additional agreement by year-end,” she said.
